In the rocky walls above him he saw a row of four-cornered white doors, from which a sort of glimmer, as from the northern lights, shot downwards through the darkness. This valley stretched in a northeastwardly direction right under Finmark, she said, and inside the white doors dwelt the old Finn Kings who had perished on the sea. Then she went and opened the nearest of these doors﻿—here, down in the salt ocean, was the last of the kings, who had capsized in the very breeze that he himself had conjured forth, but could not afterwards quell. There, on a block of stone, sat a wrinkled yellow Finn with running eyes and a polished dark-red crown. His large head rocked backwards and forwards on his withered neck, as if it were in the swirl of an ocean current. Beside him, on the same block, sat a still more shrivelled and yellow little woman, who also had a crown on, and her garments were covered with all sorts of coloured stones; she was stirring up a brew with a stick. If she only had fire beneath it, the girl told Eilert, she and her husband would very soon have dominion again over the salt sea, for the thing she was stirring about was magic stuff.

In the middle of a plain, which opened right before them at a turn of the road, stood a few houses together like a little town, and, a little further on, Eilert saw a church turned upside down, looking, with its long pointed tower, as if it were mirrored in the water. The girl explained to him that her father dwelt in these houses, and the church was one of the seven that stood in his realm, which extended all over Helgoland and Finmark. No service was held in them yet, but it would be held when the drowned bishop, who sat outside in a brown study, could only hit upon the name of the Lord that was to be served, and then all the Draugs would go to church. The bishop, she said, had been sitting and pondering the matter over these eight hundred years, so he would no doubt very soon get to the bottom of it. A hundred years ago the bishop had advised them to send up one of the Draugs to Rödö church to find out all about it; but every time the word he wanted was mentioned he couldn’t catch the sound of it. In the mountain “Kunnan” King Olaf had hung a church-bell of pure gold, and it is guarded by the first priest who ever came to Nordland, who stands there in a white chasuble.

===Chinese===
他们不时经过一条半埋在沙里的小船或大船，鱼儿在船舱的门窗里游进游出。沉船近旁徘徊着一些人影，看上去仿佛只是一缕缕青烟。引路的姑娘向他解释说，这些都是溺死者的魂灵，死时未曾得到基督徒的安葬——须得提防他们，因为这路死者心怀歹意。每逢自己的族人将要遭难，他们总事先知晓，每到那时节，他们便在冬夜里嚎出德劳格的死亡预警。

随后他们继续赶路，径直穿过一道幽深的黑谷。头顶的岩壁上，他看见一排四四方方的白门，门里泻出一种光，像北极光一般，穿过黑暗直射下来。她说，这道谷朝东北一路延伸，正通到芬马克底下；白门里住着古时葬身大海的芬恩诸王。说着她走过去，推开了最近的一扇门——在这盐海深处的，是诸王中的末代王：他那条船翻掉时所借的那阵风，正是他自己召起来、事后却再也压服不住的。一块石头上坐着一个满脸皱纹、蜡黄的芬恩老头，眼睛淌着水，头顶一顶擦得锃亮的暗红王冠。那颗大脑袋在干瘪的脖颈上摇来晃去，仿佛还困在一股洋流的漩涡里。他身旁，同一块石头上，坐着一个更干瘪、更蜡黄的小老太婆，头上也顶着冠，衣裳上缀满五色宝石；她正用一根棍子搅着一锅熬着的东西。姑娘告诉埃勒特，只要那锅底下生得起火，她和丈夫很快就能重新君临这盐海，因为她搅动的，正是巫术的料。

道路一转，眼前豁然展开一片平地，中央几座房屋聚作一处，像座小镇；再往前去，埃勒特望见一座倒立着的教堂，长长的尖塔朝下，宛如倒映在水中。姑娘向他解释说，她父亲就住在这几座房子里；那教堂是他王国中七座教堂之一，他的王国绵延在整个海尔格兰（Helgoland）与芬马克。教堂里还没有做过礼拜，但总有一天会做的——只消那位坐在外头出神的溺亡主教，能想起那位当受侍奉的主的圣名，那时节，众德劳格就都要上教堂了。她说，主教坐着参详这件事，至今已有八百年，想来不久必能参透。一百年前，主教曾建议他们派一个德劳格上到勒德厄（Rödö）教堂去，把这件事打听个明白；可每逢人家一提到他想要的那个词，他偏偏又捕捉不到那个声音。在“昆南”（Kunnan）山腹里，奥拉夫王（King Olaf）悬挂了一口纯金的教堂大钟，看守它的是头一个来到诺尔兰（Nordland）的司铎，他立在那里，身披白色祭披。

===Original===
On the day the priest rings the bell, Kunnan will become a big stone church, to which all Nordland, both above and below the sea, will resort. But time flies, and therefore all who come down here below are asked by the bishop if they can tell him that name.

At this Eilert felt very queer indeed, and he felt queerer still when he began reflecting and found, to his horror, that he also had forgotten that name.

While he stood there in thought, the girl looked at him so anxiously. It was almost as if she wanted to help him to find it and couldn’t, and with that she all at once grew deadly pale.

The Draug’s house, to which they now came, was built of boat’s keels and large pieces of wreckage, in the interstices of which grew all sorts of sea-grass and slimy green stuff. Three monstrously heavy green posts, covered with shellfish, formed the entrance, and the door consisted of planks which had sunk to the bottom and were full of clincher-nails. In the middle of it, like a knocker, was a heavy rusty iron mooring-ring, with the worn-away stump of a ship’s hawser hanging to it. When they came up to it, a large black arm stretched out and opened the door.

===Chinese===
到了司铎敲响那口钟的日子，昆南山就会化作一座石头大教堂，海上海下，整个诺尔兰都要拥到那里去。只是光阴似箭，所以凡是下到这底下的人，主教都要问上一句：能不能把那个名字告诉他。

听到这儿，埃勒特心里着实发毛；待他细一寻思，惊恐地发现自己竟也想不起那个名字，就更发毛了。

他站在那儿出神，姑娘焦灼地望着他。那神情几乎像是她想帮他找到、却又无从帮起；这么一想，她忽然变得死一样苍白。

他们这时来到的这座德劳格的宅子，是用船的龙骨和大块沉船的残料搭成的，缝隙里长满了各种海草和黏滑的绿藻。三根粗重骇人的绿柱，挂满贝类，撑成门道；门板是沉过底的厚木板，钉满密密的铆钉。门正中像一个门环似的，是一只锈得死沉的铁系缆环，上头还挂着一截磨秃了的船缆桩头。他们走到近前，一条黑色的大手臂伸出来，推开了门。
===Original===
They were now in a vaulted chamber, with fine shell-sand on the floor. In the corners lay all sorts of ropes, yarn, and boating-gear, and among them casks and barrels and various ship’s inventories. On a heap of yarn, covered by an old red-patched sail, Eilert saw the Draug, a broad-shouldered, strongly built fellow, with a glazed hat shoved back on to the top of his head, with dark-red tangled hair and beard, small tearful dogfish eyes, and a broad mouth, round which there lay for the moment a good-natured seaman’s grin. The shape of his head reminded one somewhat of the big sort of seal which is called *Klakkekal*﻿—his skin about the neck looked dark and shaggy, and the tops of his fingers grew together. He sat there with turned-down sea-boots on, and his thick grey woollen stockings reached right up to his thigh. He wore besides, plain freize clothes with bright glass buttons on his waistcoat. His spacious skin jacket was open, and round his neck he had a cheap red woollen scarf.

When Eilert came up, he made as if he would rise, and said good naturedly, “Good day, Eilert﻿—you’ve certainly had a hard time of it today! Now you can sit down, if you like, and take a little grub. You want it, I’m sure;” and with that he squirted out a jet of tobacco juice like the spouting of a whale. With one foot, which for that special purpose all at once grew extraordinarily long, he fished out of a corner, in true Nordland style, the skull of a whale to serve as a chair for Eilert, and shoved forward with his hand a long ship’s drawer full of first-rate fare. There was boiled groats with syrup, cured fish, oatcakes with butter, a large stack of flatcakes, and a multitude of the best hotel dishes besides.

The Merman bade him fall to and eat his fill, and ordered his daughter to bring out the last keg of Thronhjem aqua vitae. “Of that sort the last is always the best,” said he. When she came with it, Eilert thought he knew it again: it was his father’s, and he himself, only a couple of days before, had bought the brandy from the wholesale dealer at Kvæford; but he didn’t say anything about that now. The quid of tobacco, too, which the Draug turned somewhat impatiently in his mouth before he drank, also seemed to him wonderfully like the lead on his own line. At first it seemed to him as if he didn’t quite know how to manage with the keg﻿—his mouth was so sore; but afterwards things went along smoothly enough.

===Chinese===
他们进了一间拱顶的厅堂，地上铺着细软的贝壳沙。角落里堆着各式缆绳、纱线和船上的家什，当中还夹着桶桶罐罐和各样船用物件。一堆纱线上头盖着一张打红补丁的旧帆，埃勒特看见德劳格就坐在那上头——一条肩膀宽阔、筋骨结实的汉子，一顶油亮的高帽推在头顶后头，暗红的须发纠结成一团，一双小小的角鲨眼睛泪汪汪的，一张阔嘴巴上，这会儿正挂着一副水手和善的憨笑。他那个脑袋的形状，有点像人称克拉克卡卡尔（*Klakkekal*）的大海豹——脖颈四周的皮又黑又蓬乱，指尖彼此连生在一处。他脚上蹬着翻口的长筒水靴，厚厚的灰毛线袜直套到大腿根。此外还穿一身素色粗呢衣，背心上缀着亮玻璃扣子。宽大的皮夹克敞着怀，脖子上围一条便宜的红毛线围巾。

埃勒特走上前，他作势要站起来，和和气气地说：“你好啊，埃勒特——你今天可是真够受的！坐吧，要是乐意，就先垫补一点。你肯定缺这个；”说着，他喷出一股烟草汁，活像鲸鱼喷水。他伸出一只脚——那只脚为着这个用场，霎时间长得格外地长——从角落里勾出一具鲸鱼颅骨，地地道道的诺尔兰做派，给埃勒特当椅子；又拿手推过来一只长长的船上抽屉，满盛着一等一的上好吃食。有浇糖浆的煮麦片粥、腌鱼、抹黄油的燕麦饼、一大摞薄煎饼，此外还有数不清的顶级酒店名菜。

人鱼招呼他放开吃、吃个饱，又吩咐女儿去把最后一小桶特隆赫姆（Thronhjem）烧酒取来。“这种东西，总是最后一桶最好。”他说。她把酒取来时，埃勒特觉得眼熟：那是他父亲的桶，就在两三天前，还是他亲自从克瓦福德（Kvæford）的批发商手里买的这烈酒；不过这会儿他什么也没说。德劳格喝酒前，把嘴里那团嚼烟有些不耐烦地翻了个个儿，那烟团他也觉得眼熟得很，活脱脱像自己钓线上的铅坠。起初，他摆弄那只小桶似乎有些不得法——嘴里疼得慌；可后来就顺顺当当了。

===Original===
So they sat for some time pretty silently, and drank glass after glass, till Eilert began to think that they had had quite enough. So, when it came to his turn again, he said no, he would rather not; whereupon the Merman put the keg to his own mouth and drained it to the very dregs. Then he stretched his long arm up to the shelf, and took down another. He was now in a better humour, and began to talk of all sorts of things. But every time he laughed, Eilert felt queer, for the Draug’s mouth gaped ominously wide, and showed a greenish pointed row of teeth, with a long interval between each tooth, so that they resembled a row of boat stakes.

The Merman drained keg after keg, and with every keg he grew more communicative. With an air as if he were thinking in his own mind of something very funny, he looked at Eilert for a while and blinked his eyes. Eilert didn’t like his expression at all, for it seemed to him to say: “Now, my lad, whom I have fished up so nicely, look out for a change!” But instead of that he said, “You had a rough time of it last night, Eilert, my boy, but it wouldn’t have gone so hard with you if you hadn’t streaked the lines with corpse-mould, and refused to take my daughter to church”﻿—here he suddenly broke off, as if he had said too much, and to prevent himself from completing the sentence, he put the brandy-keg to his mouth once more. But the same instant Eilert caught his glance, and it was so full of deadly hatred that it sent a shiver right down his back.

When, after a long, long draught, he again took the keg from his mouth, the Merman was again in a good humour, and told tale after tale. He stretched himself more and more heavily out on the sail, and laughed and grinned complacently at his own narrations, the humour of which was always a wreck or a drowning. From time to time Eilert felt the breath of his laughter, and it was like a cold blast. If folks would only give up their boats, he said, he had no very great desire for the crews. It was driftwood and ship-timber that he was after, and he really couldn’t get on without them. When his stock ran out, boat or ship he *must* have, and surely nobody could blame him for it either.

With that he put the keg down empty, and became somewhat more gloomy again. He began to talk about what bad times they were for him and her. It was not as it used to be, he said. He stared blankly before him for a time, as if buried in deep thought. Then he stretched himself out backwards at full length, with feet extending right across the floor, and gasped so dreadfully that his upper and lower jaws resembled two boats’ keels facing each other. Then he dozed right off with his neck turned towards the sail.

===Chinese===
两人就这样默默地坐了一阵，一杯接一杯地喝，喝到埃勒特开始觉得实在够了。轮到他再喝时，他说不喝了，不想再喝了；人鱼便把小桶举到自己嘴边，一饮到底，涓滴不剩。随后他伸长手臂，从架子上又取下一桶。他这会儿兴头好了些，开始天南海北地谈起来。可他每笑一声，埃勒特心里就发一回毛：德劳格那张嘴咧开得不祥地宽阔，露出一排尖尖的绿牙，牙与牙之间豁着长长的空当，活像一排水下的船桩。

人鱼一桶接一桶地灌，每灌一桶，话匣子便开大一分。他带着一副心里正转着什么妙事的架势，朝埃勒特眨着眼端详了一阵。埃勒特一点儿也不喜欢他这副神情，因为那仿佛在说：“小子，我把你美美地钓上来了，可放精明着些，就要变天啦！”可他嘴里说的却是：“昨儿夜里你吃了苦头了，我的埃勒特小子；不过，你要不是往钓线上抹了尸土，又死活不肯带我女儿上教堂，本来也犯不着遭这份罪”——说到这儿他猛地打住，像是说漏了嘴，为了不让这句话说完，他把酒桶重新举到了嘴边。可就在同一瞬间，埃勒特撞上了他的目光，那目光里盛满刻骨的死恨，激得他脊背上窜过一阵寒颤。

长长一口酒咽下去，他把酒桶从嘴边拿开时，人鱼又高兴起来，一个故事接一个故事地讲。他越来越沉地摊开身子，躺平在那面帆上，对自己的讲述自得其乐地咧着嘴笑，那些故事的妙处，从来离不开沉船与溺亡。埃勒特不时感到他笑声的气息，像一阵冷风刮过。他说，只要人们肯把船交出来，船上的人手他倒不怎么惦记；他图的是浮木和船材，没了这些他实在过不下去。存货一空，船他就*非*到手不可，这总归怪不得他吧。

说着，他把空桶放下，神色又阴沉了几分。他开始念叨，如今这世道对他和她有多艰难。不比从前了，他说。他直勾勾朝前面瞪了一阵，仿佛沉入了深想。随后他把身子整个向后放平，双脚一直伸到厅堂那头，喘得可怕，上下两张颌骨活像两条相向而对的船底龙骨。接着，他脖子朝着那面帆，沉沉睡去了。

===Original===
Then the girl again stood by Eilert’s side, and bade him follow her.

They now went the same way back, and again ascended up to the skerry. Then she confided to him that the reason why her father had been so bitter against him was because he had mocked her with the taunt about church-cleansing when she had wanted to go to church﻿—the name the folks down below wanted to know might, the Merman thought, be treasured up in Eilert’s memory; but during their conversation on their way down to her father, she had perceived that he also had forgotten it. And now he must look to his life.

It would be a good deal later on in the day before the old fellow would begin inquiring about him. Till then he, Eilert, must sleep so as to have sufficient strength for his flight﻿—she would watch over him.

The girl flung her long dark hair about him like a curtain, and it seemed to him that he knew those eyes so well. He felt as if his cheek were resting against the breast of a white seabird, it was so warm and sleep-giving﻿—a single reddish feather in the middle of it recalled a dark memory. Gradually he sank off into a doze, and heard her singing a lullaby, which reminded him of the swell of the billows when it ripples up and down along the beach on a fine sunny day. It was all about how they had once been playmates together, and how later on he would have nothing to say to her. Of all she sang, however, he could only recollect the last words, which were these﻿—

===Chinese===
这时姑娘又站到埃勒特身边，叫他跟上自己。

他们顺原路返回，又上到那座礁岛。这时她才向他吐露：父亲之所以那样记恨他，是因为她想上教堂的时候，他竟拿教堂净化的挖苦话羞辱了她——底下那些人打听的那个名字，人鱼原想，说不定就珍藏在埃勒特的记忆里；可在下去见父亲的一路上，从言谈话语中她已察觉，他也把它忘了。如今他可得把性命当心了。

还得过好半天，那老家伙才会问起他来。在那之前，他埃勒特得睡一觉，攒足逃命的力气——她会守着他。

姑娘把长长的黑发像帘幕一样拢罩住他，他觉得那双眼睛自己熟识极了。他只觉自己的脸颊贴着一只白色海鸟的胸脯，那样温暖，那样催人入睡——正中一根微红的羽毛，勾起一段阴暗的往事。他渐渐沉入迷蒙，听见她唱起一支摇篮曲，那曲子让他想起晴好的艳阳天，涌浪沿着滩头起起落落的碎语。唱的全是他们从前怎样做过游伴，后来他又怎样不再理她。只是她唱的一切，他只记得最后几句，那几句是——
===Original===
> “Oh, thousands of times have we played on the shore,
 

 And caught little fishes﻿—dost mind it no more?
 

 We raced with the surf as it rolled at our feet,
 

 And the lurking old Merman we always did cheat.
> “Yes, much shalt thou think of at my lullaby,
 

 Whilst the billows do rock and the breezes do sigh.
 

 Who sits now and weeps o’er thy cheeks? It is she
 

 Who gave thee her soul, and whose soul lived in thee.
> “But once as an eider-duck homeward I came
 

 Thou didst lie ’neath a rock, with thy rifle didst aim;
 

 In my breast thou didst strike me; the blood thou dost see
 

 Is the mark that I bear, oh! beloved one, of thee.”

===Chinese===
> “哦，千百次我们在岸边玩耍，
 

 捉着小鱼儿——你可不再记起？
 

 我们追着卷上脚边的浪头飞跑，
 

 潜伏的老人鱼总被我们瞒掉。
> “听着这摇篮曲，你会想起许多，
 

 当波涛摇荡，当清风叹息。
 

 如今坐着为你的脸颊垂泪的是谁？是她，
 

 她把灵魂给了你，她的灵魂也曾活在你身体里。
> “可有一次我化作绒鸭归家，
 

 你正卧在岩下，端枪瞄准；
 

 你射中了我的胸膛；你看见的这滴血，
 

 便是我身上带着的，啊！亲爱的人，你的印记。”

===Original===
Then it seemed to Eilert as if she sat and wept over him, and that, from time to time, a drop like a splash of seawater fell upon his cheek. He felt now that he loved her so dearly.

The next moment he again became uneasy. He fancied that right up to the skerry came a whale, which said that he, Eilert, must now make haste; and when he stood on its back he stuck the shaft of an oar down its nostril, to prevent it from shooting beneath the sea again. He perceived that in this way the whale could be steered accordingly as he turned the oar to the right or left; and now they coasted the whole land of Finmark at such a rate that the huge mountain islands shot by them like little rocks. Behind him he saw the Draug in his half-boat, and he was going so swiftly that the foam stood mid-mast high. Shortly afterwards he was again lying on the skerry, and the lass smiled so blithely; she bent over him and said, “It is I, Eilert.”

With that he awoke, and saw that the sunbeams were running over the wet skerry, and the Mermaid was still sitting by his side. But presently the whole thing changed before his eyes. It was the sun shining through the windowpanes, on a bed in the Finn’s hut, and by his side sat the Finn girl supporting his back, for they thought he was about to die. He had lain there delirious for six weeks, ever since the Finn had rescued him after capsizing, and this was his first moment of consciousness.

After that it seemed to him that he had never heard anything so absurd and presumptuous as the twaddle that would fix a stigma of shame or contempt on Finn blood, and the same spring he and the Finn girl Zilla were betrothed, and in the autumn they were married.

There were Finns in the bridal procession, and perhaps many said a little more about that than they need have done; but everyone at the wedding agreed that the fiddler, who was also a Finn, was the best fiddler in the whole parish, and the bride the prettiest girl.

===Chinese===
随后，埃勒特仿佛觉得她坐在身旁为自己哭泣，还不时有一滴像海水溅落的水珠，掉在他的脸颊上。他此刻感到，自己是那样深爱着她。

下一刻，他又不安起来。他恍惚觉得，一头鲸鱼径直游到礁岛跟前，说埃勒特这会儿得赶紧了；待他站上鲸背，便把一支船桨的桨杆插进它的鼻孔，免得它又一头扎回海里去。他发觉，这样便能驾驭鲸鱼：桨往右拧它往右，往左拧它往左；他们沿岸掠过整个芬马克，速度快得那些大山一般的岛子，像小石子似的从身边掠过去。他看见德劳格驾着他那半条船跟在后头，来势疾得翻腾的白沫蹿起半桅高。不多时，他又躺在了礁岛上，姑娘笑得那样欢，俯下身对他说：“是我呀，埃勒特。”

话音落下，他醒了，看见阳光淌过湿漉漉的礁石，人鱼姑娘仍坐在他身边。可转眼间，这一切在他眼前变了样：原来是太阳照进窗玻璃，照在芬恩人小屋的一张床上，他身边坐着那芬恩姑娘，扶撑着他的后背——他们都当他要死了。自打翻船后被那芬恩人救起，他一直躺在这里，高烧昏迷、说着胡话，已有六个星期，此刻是他神志清醒的头一刻。

打那以后，他只觉得这辈子再没听见过比这更荒唐、更狂悖的蠢话——竟要把耻辱或轻蔑的烙印，烙在芬恩之血上。当年春天，他同芬恩姑娘齐拉订了亲，到秋天，两人成了婚。

送亲的队伍里有芬恩人，或许不少人对此多说了几句不必要的话；但婚宴上人人一致公认：那位拉提琴的——他也是个芬恩人——是全教区最好的琴手，新娘是最俊俏的姑娘。

## Foreword / 前言

===Original===
This edition of Jonas Lie’s *Short Fiction* was produced from two translations. “The Grey” was translated by *Littel’s Living Age* and published in 1879. “The Fisherman and the Draug,” “Jack of Sjöholm and the Gan-Finn,” “Tug of War,” “ ‘The Earth Draws,’ ” “The Cormorants of Andvaer,” “Isaac and the Parson of Brönö,” “The Wind-Gnome,” “The Huldrefish,” “Finn-Blood,” “The Homestead Westward in the Blue Mountains” and “ ‘It’s Me’ ” were translated by Robert Nisbet Bain and published in *Weird Tales from Northern Seas* in 1893.

===Chinese===
本版约纳斯·利（Jonas Lie）的《短篇集》由两种译本编成。《灰马》一篇由《利特尔氏活时代》（Littel’s Living Age）译出，1879 年发表。《渔夫与德劳格》《舍霍尔姆的杰克与甘-芬恩》《拔河》《“大地在拉扯”》《安德沃尔的鸬鹚》《艾萨克与布伦讷的牧师》《风精》《哈尔德鱼》《芬恩之血》《蓝山以西的农庄》与《“是我”》诸篇，均由罗伯特·尼斯贝特·贝恩（Robert Nisbet Bain）译出，1893 年收录于《北方诸海怪谈》（Weird Tales from Northern Seas）出版。

===Original===
Hendrik Kaiber
Santa Maria, Brazil, January 2025

===Chinese===
亨德里克·凯贝尔
巴西圣玛丽亚，2025 年 1 月

## Isaac and the Parson of Brönö / 艾萨克与布伦讷的牧师

===Original===

In Helgeland there was once a fisherman called Isaac. One day when he was out halibut fishing he felt something heavy on the lines. He drew up, and, lo! there was a sea-boot.

“That *was* a rum ’un!” said he, and he sat there a long time looking at it.

It looked just as if it might be the boot of his brother who had gone down in the great storm last winter on his way home from fishing.

There was still something *inside* the boot too, but he durst not look to see what it was, nor did he exactly know what to do with the sea-boot either.

He didn’t want to take it home and frighten his mother, nor did he quite fancy chucking it back into the sea again; so he made up his mind to go to the parson of Brönö, and beg him to bury it in a Christian way.

===Chinese===

从前，海尔格兰（Helgeland）有个渔夫，名叫艾萨克（Isaac）。一天他出海钓大比目鱼，觉得钓绳上沉甸甸的。他拽上来一看，嘿！竟是一只长筒水靴。

“这*可*真是个稀罕玩意儿！”他说，坐在那儿盯着它瞧了半天。

瞧那模样，很可能就是他兄弟的靴子——去年冬天，兄弟打鱼归途中遇上了那场大风暴，沉进了海里。

靴子里头还留着*东西*呢，可他不敢去看那究竟是什么，也不大晓得该拿这只长筒水靴怎么办。

他不想把它带回家吓着母亲，也不大舍得把它再扔回海里去；于是拿定主意，去见布伦讷（Brönö）的牧师，求他按基督教的仪式把它安葬了。

===Original===

“But I can’t bury a sea-boot,” quoth the parson.

The fellow scratched his head. “Na, na!” said he.

Then he wanted to know how much there ought to be of a human body before it could have the benefit of Christian burial.

“That I cannot exactly tell you,” said the parson; “a tooth, or a finger, or hair clippings is not enough to read the burial service over. Anyhow, there ought to be so much remaining that one can see that a soul has been in it. But to read Holy Scripture over a toe or two in a sea-boot! Oh, no! that would never do!”

===Chinese===

“可我不能安葬一只长筒水靴啊。”牧师道。

汉子挠了挠头。“不，不！”他说。

接着他又想知道，一个人的尸身总得剩下多少，才够格享受基督教的安葬。

“这我可没法跟你说准，”牧师说，“一颗牙、一根手指，或是几绺剪下来的头发，都不足以让人对着诵念安葬经文。不管怎么说，总得剩下那么多，让人看得出里头曾住过一个灵魂。可要对着一两根长筒水靴里的脚趾念圣书！哦，不行！那可万万使不得！”

===Original===

But Isaac watched his opportunity, and managed to get the sea-boot into the churchyard on the sly, all the same.

And home he went.

It seemed to him that he had done the best he could. It was better, after all, that *something* of his brother should lie so near God’s house than that he should have heaved the boot back into the black sea again.

But, towards autumn, it so happened that, as he lay out among the skerries on the lookout for seals, and the ebb-tide drove masses of tangled seaweed towards him, he fished up a knife-belt and an empty sheath with his oar-blade.

===Chinese===

可艾萨克还是瞅准了空子，悄悄把长筒水靴弄进了教堂墓园。

随后他就回家去了。

在他看来，自己已尽了全力。兄弟身上好歹有*一样什么*安眠在离上帝殿堂这么近的地方，总强过把靴子重新抛回黑色的海里去。

可是到了快入秋的时候，偏巧有那么一回，他埋伏在外面的礁岛群间守候海豹，退潮把大团大团纠结的海藻朝他推来，他用桨叶捞起了一条刀带和一只空刀鞘。

===Original===

He recognised them at once as his brother’s.

The tarred wire covering of the sheath had been loosened and bleached by the sea; and he remembered quite well how, when his brother had sat and cobbled away at this sheath, he had chatted and argued with him about the leather for his belt which he had taken from an old horse which they had lately killed.

They had bought the buckle together over at the storekeeper’s on the Saturday, and mother had sold bilberries, and capercailzies, and three pounds of wool. They had got a little tipsy, and had had such fun with the old fishwife at the headland, who had used a bast-mat for a sail.

So he took the belt away with him, and said nothing about it. It was no good giving pain to no purpose, thought he.

===Chinese===

他一眼就认出，这些都是兄弟的物件。

刀鞘外缠的涂柏油铁丝已被海水泡松、漂得发白；他还清清楚楚记得，兄弟坐在那儿缝补这只刀鞘的工夫，怎样跟他闲谈拌嘴，说那条腰带用的皮子，是从他们前不久宰掉的一匹老马身上剥来的。

皮带扣是那个礼拜六他们俩一道在杂货铺买的，母亲还卖了越橘、松鸡和三磅羊毛。两人喝得微微有点上头，还跟岬角上那位拿树皮席子当帆使的老渔婆好一阵取乐。

于是他把刀带随身带走了，只字未提。白白惹人伤心没有好处，他想。

===Original===

But the longer the winter lasted the more he bothered himself with odd notions about what the parson had said. And he knew not what he should do in case he came upon something else, such as another boot, or something that a squid, or a fish, or a crab, or a Greenland shark might have bitten off. He began to be really afraid of rowing out in the sea there among the skerries.

And yet, for all that, it was as though he were constantly being drawn thither by the hope of finding, perhaps, so much of the remains as might show the parson where the soul had been, and so move him to give them a Christian burial.

He took to walking about all by himself in a brown study.

And then, too, he had such nasty dreams.

===Chinese===

然而冬天拖得越久，他就越为牧师那番话胡思乱想、心神不宁。倘若再撞见别的东西可怎么好——比如又一只靴子，或是被乌贼、鱼、螃蟹或者格陵兰睡鲨咬剩下的什么。他当真开始害怕划船出海，到那片礁岛群里去了。

然而尽管如此，他又仿佛总被一种指望牵着往那儿去：说不定能寻着足够多的遗骸，让牧师看明白灵魂曾在何处，从而动了心，肯给它们一场基督教的安葬。

他变得独来独往，一个人走来走去，想得出了神。

再说，他还净做些可怕的梦。

===Original===

His door flew open in the middle of the night and let in a cold sea-blast, and it seemed to him as if his brother were limping about the room, and yelling that he must have his foot again, the Draugs were pulling and twisting him about so.

For hours and hours he stood over his work without laying a hand to it, and blankly staring at the fifth wall.33

At last he felt as if he were really going out of his wits, because of the great responsibility he had taken upon himself by burying the foot in the churchyard.

===Chinese===

深更半夜，他的房门猛地敞开，灌进一股冰冷的海风，他仿佛看见兄弟一瘸一拐地在屋里打转，嚎叫着非要讨回他的脚不可——德劳格（Draug）们把他拽来拧去，折腾得苦。

他一连好几个钟头站在活计跟前，手也不动一下，只呆呆地瞪着那第五面墙 33。

末了他觉得自己真要发疯了，全因为把那只脚埋进了教堂墓园，独自扛下这副天大的干系。

===Original===

He didn’t want to pitch it into the sea again, but it couldn’t lie in the churchyard either.

It was borne in upon him so clearly that his brother could not be among the blessed, and he kept going about and thinking of all that might be lying and drifting and floating about among the skerries.

So he took it upon himself to dredge there, and lay out by the seashore with ropes and dredging gear. But all that he dredged up was sea-wrack, and weeds, and starfish, and like rubbish.

===Chinese===

他不想把它再抛回海里，可它也不能再躺在教堂墓园里了。

他心里明明白白地认定：兄弟进不了极乐之境；于是他不停地走动，盘算着还有多少东西可能躺在礁岛群间，随波逐流、四处漂浮。

于是他横下心来，带着绳索和打捞家伙守在海边，在那片水域打捞。可捞上来的尽是海草、海藻、海星，诸如此类的破烂。

===Original===

One evening as he sat out there by the rocks trying his luck at fishing, and the line with the stone and all the hooks upon it shot down over the boat’s side, the last of the hooks caught in one of his eyes, and right to the bottom went the eye.

There was no use dredging for *that*, and he could see to row home very well without it.

In the night he lay with a bandage over his eye, wakeful for pain, and he thought and thought till things looked as black as they could be to him. Was there ever anyone in the world in such a hobble as he?

===Chinese===

一天傍晚，他坐在外头礁石边碰运气钓鱼，钓绳连着坠石和满满一排钓钩从船舷一溜滑下去，最末一枚钩子钩住了他的一只眼睛，那只眼睛便径直沉进了海底。

那*东西*打捞也是白搭，好歹少了一只眼，划船回家还不成问题。

夜里他蒙着一只眼躺着，疼得睡不着，翻来覆去地想，越想越觉得万事漆黑一片。世上可曾有谁落到过他这样进退两难的境地？

===Original===

All at once such an odd thing happened.

He thought he was looking about him, deep down in the sea, and he saw the fishes flitting and snapping about among the sea-wrack and seaweeds round about the fishing line. They bit at the bait, and wriggled and tried to slip off, first a cod, and then a ling, and then a dogfish. Last of all, a haddock came and stood still there, and chewed the water a little as if it were tasting before swallowing it.

And he saw there what he couldn’t take his eyes off. It looked like the back of a man in leather clothes, with one sleeve caught beneath the grapnel of a Fembøring.34

Then a heavy white halibut came up and gulped down the hook, and it became pitch dark.

===Chinese===

突然间，一桩怪事发生了。

他觉得自己正在深海里四下张望，看见鱼儿在钓绳周围的海草海藻间穿梭啄食。它们咬住鱼饵，扭着身子想挣脱开去：先是一条鳕鱼，接着一条狼鳕，再一条角鲨。最后来了一条黑线鳕，在那儿停住，还稍稍咂了咂水，仿佛吞咽之前先品一品滋味。

他在那里看见了让他挪不开眼的东西。那看上去像个穿皮衣的男人的背影，一只袖子被一艘芬伯艇（Fembøring）34 的四爪锚压在底下。

随后一条肥大的白色大比目鱼游上来，一口吞了钓钩，四下便变得漆黑。

===Original===

“You must let the big halibut slip off again when you pull up tomorrow,” something said, “the hook tears my mouth so. ’Tis of no use searching except in the evening, when the tide in the sound is on the ebb.”

Next day he went off, and took a piece of a tombstone from the churchyard to dredge the bottom with; and in the evening, when the tide had turned, he lay out in the sound again and searched.

Immediately he hauled up the grapnel of a Fembøring, the hooks of which were clinging to a leather fisherman’s jacket, with the remains of an arm in it.

The fishes had got as much as they could of it out of the leather jacket.

===Chinese===

“明天你往上拉的时候，得让那条大比目鱼再脱了钩，”不知什么东西说道，“钩子把我的嘴扯得好疼。只有傍晚海峡退潮的时候找，才管用。”

第二天他出发了，从教堂墓园里取了块墓碑石，充作捞底的坠子；傍晚潮水转向时，他又卧在海峡里打捞搜寻。

他立时捞上来一艘芬伯艇的四爪锚，锚钩上挂着一件渔人的皮上衣，里头还残着半条胳膊。

鱼群已经把皮上衣里能掏到的东西都掏光了。

===Original===

Off to the parson he rowed straightway.

“What! read the service over a washed-out old leather jacket!” cried the parson of Brönö.

“I’ll throw the sea-boot into the bargain,” answered Isaac.

“Waifs and strays and sea salvage should be advertised in the church porch,” thundered the parson.

===Chinese===

他径直划船去找牧师。

“什么！给一件泡得发白的旧皮上衣念安葬经文！”布伦讷的牧师嚷道。

“我把那只长筒水靴一并奉送。”艾萨克答道。

“无主的漂失物和海上打捞品，应当贴到教堂门廊里去招领。”牧师雷鸣般喝道。

===Original===

Then Isaac looked straight into the parson’s face.

“The sea-boot has been heavy enough on my conscience,” said he; “and I’m sure I don’t want to be saddled with the leather jacket as well.”

“I tell you I don’t mean to cast consecrated earth to the winds,” said the parson; he was getting wroth.

Isaac scratched his head again. “Na, na!” said he.

===Chinese===

这时艾萨克直直地望进牧师的眼睛。

“那只长筒水靴压在我良心上已经够沉了，”他说，“这皮上衣再驮上身，我是万万不情愿的。”

“我告诉你，我可不打算把祝圣过的圣土抛撒到风里去。”牧师说；他恼起来了。

艾萨克又挠了挠头。“不，不！”他说。

===Original===

And with that he had to be content and go home.

But Isaac had neither rest nor repose, there lay such a grievous load upon him.

In the night time he again saw the big white halibut. It was going round and round so slowly and sadly in the selfsame circle at the bottom of the sea. It was just as if some invisible sort of netting was all round it, and the whole time it was striving to slip through the meshes.

Isaac lay there, and gazed and gazed till his blind eye ached again.

===Chinese===

事已至此，他也只得认了，回家去。

可艾萨克不得安生，心头压着一副沉甸甸的担子。

夜里他又看见了那条肥大的白色大比目鱼。它在海底同一个圈子里一圈又一圈地游，游得那么迟缓、那么哀伤。它四周围着仿佛一张看不见的网，它一个劲儿地想钻出网眼去。

艾萨克躺在那儿，望啊望啊，望到那只瞎眼又疼了起来。

===Original===

No sooner was he out dredging next day, and had let down the ropes, than an ugly heavy squid came up, and spouted up a black jet right in front of him.

But one evening he let the boat drive, as the current chose to take it, outside the skerries, but within the islands. At last it stopped at a certain spot, as if it were moored fast, and there it grew wondrously still; there was not a bird in the air or a sign of life in the sea.

All at once up came a big bubble right in front of the jib, and as it burst he heard a deep heavy sigh.

But Isaac had his own opinion about what he had seen.

===Chinese===

第二天他刚出去打捞，绳索才放下去，一只丑陋的大乌贼便浮了上来，冲着他喷出一股黑水。

可是有一天傍晚，他任凭小船随潮流漂去，漂到礁岛群之外、诸岛之内。最后船在一处停了下来，仿佛被牢牢拴住，四下静得出奇；空中不见一只飞鸟，海面没有一丝生息。

猛然间，艏帆跟前冒起一个大水泡，水泡破开时，他听见一声深沉滞重的叹息。

不过，对亲眼所见的一切，艾萨克自有主张。

===Original===

“And the parson of Brönö shall see to the funeral too, or I’ll know the reason why,” said he.

From henceforth it was bruited abroad that he had second sight, and saw many things about him which were hid from other folks.

He could tell exactly where the fish were to be found thick together by the sea-banks, and where they were not; and whenever they asked him about such things, he would say﻿—

“If I don’t know it, my brother does.”

===Chinese===

“布伦讷的牧师也得来照管这场葬礼，不然我倒要问出个所以然来。”他说。

打这时起，风声传了出去，说他生有阴阳眼（second sight），看得见身边许多旁人望不见的东西。

哪片海底沙坎上鱼群扎堆、哪片没有，他说得一分不差；每逢有人拿这类事问他，他总是说——

“我要是不知道，我兄弟知道。”

===Original===

Now one day it chanced that the parson of Brönö had to go out along the coast on a pious errand, and Isaac was one of them who had to row him thither.

Off they went with a rattling good breeze.

The parson got quickly there, and was not very long about his business, for next day he had to hold divine service in his own parish church.

“The firth seems to me a bit roughish,” said he, “and ’tis getting towards evening; but as we have come hither, I should think we could get back again also.”

===Chinese===

且说有一天，布伦讷的牧师恰巧要沿海外出办一桩敬神的差事，艾萨克也在奉命为他摇船送行的人之列。

他们乘着一阵畅快的劲风出发了。

牧师很快到了那儿，事情也没耽搁多久，因为第二天他还得回自己教区的教堂主持礼拜。

“依我看，峡湾里的浪头有点儿凶，”他说，“天也快晚了；不过既然来了，我想我们总也能回去。”

===Original===

They had not got very far on their homeward journey when the rising gale began to whistle and whine, so that they had to take in four clews.35

And away they went, with the sea-scud and the snowflakes flying about their ears, while the waxing rollers rose big as houses.

The parson of Brönö had never been out in such weather before. They sailed right into the rollers, and they sailed out again.

Soon it became black night.

===Chinese===

归程还没走出多远，越刮越猛的风暴就呼啸哀号起来，他们不得不收拢了四道帆脚 35。

船一路飞驰，浪花飞沫和雪片绕着耳朵乱舞，越涌越高的大浪像房屋一样立了起来。

布伦讷的牧师从没在这样的大风浪里出过海。船一头扎进浪墙，又一头钻了出来。

不久便成了漆黑的夜。

===Original===

The sea shone like mountain snowfields, and the showers of snow and spray rather waxed than waned.

Isaac had just taken in the fifth clew also when one of the planks amidships gave way, so that the sea foamed in, and the parson of Brönö and the crew leaped upon the upper deck, and bawled out that the boat was going down.

“I don’t think she’ll founder this voyage,” said Isaac; and he remained sitting where he was at the rudder.

===Chinese===

海面亮得像山间的雪原，风雪和浪沫一阵紧似一阵，不见减弱。

艾萨克刚把第五道帆脚也收拢，船中部一块船板便裂开了，海水夹着泡沫涌进来，布伦讷的牧师和船员们跳上高处的甲板，大喊船要沉了。

“我看她这一趟还沉不了。”艾萨克说；他仍旧坐在舵旁原地不动。

===Original===

But as the moon peeped forth from behind a hail-shower, they saw that a strange foremastman was standing in the scuppers, and baling the water out of the boat as fast as it poured in.

“I didn’t know that I had hired that fellow yonder,” said the parson of Brönö; “he seems to me to be baling with a sea-boot; and it also seems to me as if he had neither breeches nor skin upon his legs, and the upper part of him is neither more nor less than an empty fluttering leather jacket.”

“Parson has seen him before, I think,” said Isaac.

Then the parson of Brönö grew angry.

===Chinese===

可是当月亮从一阵冰雹后面探出脸来，他们看见一个陌生的前桅水手站在船底水沟里，船里进多少水，他就往外舀多少。

“我不知道自己几时雇过那边那个伙计，”布伦讷的牧师说，“我看他好像正拿一只长筒水靴舀水；我还看他两条腿上好像既没有裤子也没有皮肉，上半身不多不少，正是一件空荡荡、随风扑打的皮上衣。”

“牧师从前见过他吧，我想。”艾萨克说。

布伦讷的牧师这下恼了。

===Original===

“By virtue of my sacred office,” said he, “I adjure him to depart from amidships.”

“Na, na!” answered Isaac; “and can parson also answer for the plank that has burst?”

Then the parson bethought him of the evil case he was in.

“The man seems to me mortally strong, and we have great need of him,” said he; “nor is it any great sin, methinks, to help a servant of God’s over the sea. But I should like to know what he wants in return.”

===Chinese===

“凭我这圣职的权柄，”他说，“我勒令他离开船中部。”

“不，不！”艾萨克答道，“再说，牧师还能替那块爆裂的船板作保吗？”

牧师这才想起自己眼下的险境。

“依我看，这人厉害得吓人，我们还真缺他不得，”他说，“再说，依我想，帮上帝的仆人渡海也算不得什么大罪。不过我倒想知道，他要什么作报答。”

===Original===

The billows burst, and the blast howled around him.

“Only some two or three shovels of earth on a rotten sea-boot and a mouldy skin-jacket,” said Isaac.

“If you’re able to gad about again here below, I suppose there’s nothing against your being able to enter into bliss again, for all that I know,” bawled the parson of Brönö; “and you shall have your shovelfuls of earth into the bargain.”

Just as he said this, the water within the skerries all at once became quite smooth, and the parson’s boat drove high and dry upon the sandbank, so that the mast cracked.

===Chinese===

巨浪迸裂，狂风在他四周呼啸。

“不过是往一只烂掉的长筒水靴和一件发霉的皮上衣上，撒两三锹土罢了。”艾萨克说。

“你既然还能在这下头到处游荡，就我所知，想必也没有什么拦着你重新进天堂享福，”布伦讷的牧师高声喊道，“那几锹土也一并给你就是。”

话音刚落，礁岛群之内的水面霎时平静下来，牧师的船被高高冲上沙洲，搁浅在那里，桅杆咔嚓一声折断了。

## “It’s Me” / “是我”

===Original===

They had chatted so long about the lasses down in the valley; and what a fine time they had of it there, that Gygra’s43 daughter grew sick and tired of it all, and began to heave rocks against the mountain side. She was bent upon taking service in the valley below, said she.

“Then go down to the ground gnome first, and grind thy nose down, and tidy thyself up a bit, and stick a comb in thy hair instead of an iron rake,” said the dwellers in the mountains.

So Gygra’s daughter tramped along in the middle of the river, till the foss steamed and the storm whirled round about her. Down she went to the ground gnome, and was scoured and scrubbed and combed out finely.

One evening a large-limbed coarse-grained wench stepped into the general-dealer’s kitchen, and asked if she could be taken into service.

“You must be cook, then,” said Madame.44 It seemed to her that the wench was one who would stir the porridge finely, and would make no bones about a little extra wood-chopping and tub-washing. So they took her on.

===Chinese===

山里的众人把山下谷地里的姑娘们念叨了又念叨，念叨她们在那儿日子过得多么滋润，念叨得女巨人吉格拉（Gygra）43的女儿心烦透顶，便抓起石块朝山坡上砸去。她说，她拿定主意要下山到谷地里去帮工。

“那就先下去找土精（ground gnome），把你那鼻子磨平些，把自己拾掇干净点，头上插把木梳，别再插那把铁耙子。”山里的住户们说。

于是吉格拉的女儿顺着河心一路蹚了下去，蹚得瀑布腾起白汽，风暴在她四周打旋。她下到土精那里，被好生搓洗刷刮了一番，头发也细细梳通了。

一天傍晚，一个手脚粗大、皮肉粗粝的大个子丫头走进杂货商（general dealer）家的厨房，问能不能收她帮工。

“那你就当厨娘吧。”太太（Madame）44 说。在她看来，这丫头搅起粥来准是一把好手，多劈点柴、多洗几盆衣裳，也断不会有个不情愿。于是他们便把她留下了。

===Original===

She was a roughish colt, and her ways were roughish too. The first time she carried in a load of wood, she shoved so violently against the kitchen door that she burst its hinges. And however many times the carpenter might mend the door, it always remained hingeless, for she burst it open with her foot every time she brought in wood.

When she washed up, too, heaps and heaps of pots and pans were piled up higgledy-piggledy from meal to meal, so that the kitchen shelves and tables could hold no more, and bustle about as she might, they never seemed to grow less.

Nor had her mistress a much better opinion of her scouring.

When Toad, for so they called her, set to work with the sand-brush, and scrubbed with all her might, the wooden, tin, and pewter vessels would no doubt have looked downright bonny if they hadn’t broken to bits beneath her hands. And when her mistress tried to show her how it ought to be done, she only gasped and gaped.

Such sets of cracked cups, and such rows of chipped and handleless jugs and dishes, had never before been seen in that kitchen.

===Chinese===

她是匹粗野的生马驹，做派也粗野。头一回往屋里搬柴，她把厨房门撞得那样猛，竟把门上的铰链都撞脱了。木匠把门修了多少回也没用，那门永远是没铰链的——每逢她搬柴进来，总是一脚把门踹开。

她刷洗碗碟时也一样：一顿饭接一顿饭，锅碗瓢盆乱七八糟越堆越高，厨房的架子呀桌子呀都搁不下了；任她怎么忙前忙后，那堆东西仿佛总也不见少。

至于她擦洗家什的手艺，太太也没多看好几分。

蟾蜍（Toad）——大家都这么叫她——一拿起沙刷使足了劲擦洗，那些木器、锡器、白镴器皿本该擦得体面锃亮，可惜全在她手里碎成了片。太太想教她该怎么个洗法，她只管张着嘴喘气发怔。

那间厨房里，从没见过那么多成套的裂纹杯子，那么多磕了口、没了把、一溜排开的壶壶罐罐。

===Original===

And then, too, she ate as much as all the other servants put together.

So her mistress complained to her master, and said that the sooner they were well quit of her the better.

Out into the kitchen went the general dealer straightway. He was quite red in the face, and flung open the kitchen-door till it creaked again. He would let her know, he said, that she was not there to only stand with her back to the fire and warm her dirty self.

Now when he saw the lazy sluttish beast lounging over the kitchen bench and doing nothing but gape through the windowpanes at his boats, which lay down by the bridge laden with train-oil, he was downright furious. “Pack yourself off this instant!” said he.

But Toad showed her teeth, and grinned and blinked up at him, and said that as master himself had come into the kitchen, he should see that she did not eat his bread for nothing.

===Chinese===

再说，她一个人吃的，抵得上其余所有佣人加在一起。

于是太太向老爷诉苦，说这丫头越早打发走越好。

杂货商立刻动身进了厨房。他满脸涨得通红，把厨房门猛地一甩，甩得门又吱呀直叫。他说，得让她明白明白，她在这儿可不是光知道背朝炉火、烘她那身脏皮囊的。

待他瞧见那又懒又邋遢的畜生正歪在厨房长凳上，什么活儿也不干，只顾隔着窗玻璃呆望他那些泊在桥边、满载鲸油（train-oil）的船，他简直火透了。“马上给我滚！”他说。

可蟾蜍朝他龇出牙来，眯着眼嘻嘻一笑，说既然老爷自己进了厨房，那就该亲眼瞧瞧，她吃他的面包可没有白吃。

===Original===

Then she slouched down to the boats, and snorted back at him with her arm before her face. Before anyone could guess what she was after, she had one of the heavy hogsheads of train-oil on her back.

And back she came through the kitchen door, all smirking and smiling, and begged father to be so good as to tell her where she was to put it.

He simply stood and gaped at her. Such a thing he had never seen before.

And hogshead after hogshead she carried from the boat right up into the shop.

The general dealer laughed till he quite gasped for breath, and slapped his thighs so far as his big belly would let him reach them.

Nor was he sparing of compliments.

===Chinese===

说罢她便趿拉着下到船边，用胳膊挡在脸前，回头朝他哼出一声响鼻。众人还没猜出她要干什么，一只沉甸甸的鲸油大桶已经落在她背上了。

她又从厨房门走了回来，满脸堆着笑，央求“老爹”行个好，告诉她这桶该搁到哪儿。

他只是站在那儿，冲她目瞪口呆。这样的事他从来没有见过。

于是一桶接着一桶，她把鲸油从船上一直背进了铺子。

杂货商笑得喘不上气来，伸手去拍大腿——肚子太大，够得着哪儿就拍哪儿。

夸奖的话，他也没有省着。

===Original===

And into the dwelling-room he rushed almost as quickly as he had rushed out of it.

“Mother has no idea what a capital wench she has got,” said he.

But, ever after that, she put her hand to nothing, nay, not so much as to drive a wooden peg into the wall, and if someone else hadn’t warmed up a thing or two now and then, there would have been very little to eat in the house. It was as much as they could do to get her away from the fireside at meal times.

When her mistress complained about it, her master said that she oughtn’t to expect too much. The lass surely required a little rest now and again, after carrying such drayman’s loads as she did.

But Toad always had an ogle and a grin ready at such times as the general dealer came through the door from the shop. Then she grew quick and lively enough, and went on all sorts of errands, whether it was with the bucket to the spring or to the storehouse for bread. And when she saw that her mistress was out of the way, she took it upon herself to do exactly as she liked, both in this and in that.

===Chinese===

接着他一头冲进起居室，进去几乎跟他冲出来一样快。

“孩子他妈还不知道，咱们得了个多了不得的丫头。”他说。

可打那以后，她什么活儿都不沾手了，连往墙上钉根木钉都不肯动手；要不是旁人不时热上一两样吃的，家里简直要没的吃了。每顿开饭，要把她从炉边支开，都得费好大的劲。

太太为这事抱怨，老爷却说她也别指望太多。这丫头驮的是赶车汉一般的重担，想必总还得歇上一歇。

可每逢杂货商从铺子那头跨进门来，蟾蜍总有一记媚眼、一脸嬉笑候着他。这时她便手脚麻利极了，跑腿当差样样来，提桶去泉边打水也行，上库房取面包也行。而只要瞧见太太不在跟前，她便自作主张，事事都由着自己的性子来。

===Original===

No sooner was the pot hung on the pothook, than she would slip away with a big saucer and fetch syrup from the shop. And she would flounce down before the porridge dish and gobble to her heart’s content. If any of her fellow-servants claimed an equal share, she would simply answer, “It’s me!”

They dared not rebel. Since the day she had taken up the hogsheads of train-oil, they knew that she had master on her side.

But her mistress was not slow to mark the diminishing both of the syrup-pot and the powdered sugar, and she perceived also in which direction the gingerbreads and all the butter and bacon went. For out the wench would come, munching rye cakes and licking the syrup from her fingers.

And she grew as round and thick and fat as if she would burst.

When her mistress took away and kept the key, Toad would poke her head into the parlour door, and ogle and writhe at the general dealer, and ask if there was anything to carry up to the storeroom. And then he would go to the window and watch her as she lifted and carried kegs of fish and casks of sugar and sacks of meal.

===Chinese===

锅刚挂上锅钩，她就端着个大碟子溜出去，到铺子里舀糖浆。随后她一屁股坐到粥盆跟前，敞开肚皮大嚼大咽。哪个同伴要来讨一份，她只回一句：“是我！”

谁也不敢作声。打她搬起那一桶桶鲸油的那天起，大家就知道老爷是站在她那头的。

可糖浆罐和糖粉怎么日渐见了底，太太很快就察觉了；姜饼、全部的黄油和腌肉都去了哪个方向，她也看得明白。因为那丫头从铺子里出来时，嘴里嚼着黑麦饼，手指上的糖浆还舔得香甜。

她自己也吃得浑身滚圆、又粗又肥，眼看就要胀破了似的。

太太把钥匙收走自己掌管，蟾蜍便把脑袋探进客厅门，朝杂货商挤眉弄眼、扭腰摆胯，问有没有什么东西要搬上库房去。他便走到窗前，看她搬起一桶桶鱼、一桶桶糖、一袋袋面粉，扛进扛出。

===Original===

He laughed till he coughed again, and, wiping the sweat from his forehead, would bellow all over the place﻿—“Can any one of my labouring men carry loads like Toad can?”

And when her master came home, dripping wet and benumbed with cold, from his first autumn voyage, it was Toad who was first and foremost to meet him and unbutton his oilskin jacket for him, and undo his sou’wester, and help him off with his long sea-boots.

He shivered and shook; but she was not slow to wring out his wet stockings for him, and fetch no end of birch bark and huge logs. Then she made up a regular bonfire in the fireplace, and placed him cosily in the chimney corner.

Madame came to give her husband some warm ale posset; but she was so annoyed to see the wench whisking and bustling about him, that she went up into the parlour and howled with rage.

Early in the morning, the general dealer bawled and shouted downstairs for his long worsted stockings. They could hear that he was peevish and cross because he had to put on his sea-jacket and cramped water-boots, and go out again into the foul weather.

===Chinese===

他笑得又咳嗽起来，抹着额头上的汗，满屋子大声嚷嚷——“我那些卖力气的伙计，有哪个能像蟾蜍这样扛东西？”

老爷头一趟秋航回家时，浑身滴着水，冻得都僵了；头一个抢上去迎接的正是蟾蜍：替他解开油布外套的扣子，摘下他的油布帽，再帮他褪下那双长筒水靴。

他抖个不住；她却手脚飞快，替他绞干湿袜子，搬来没完没了的桦树皮和大块柴火，在壁炉里架起一堆像样的篝火，把他舒舒服服安顿在烟囱角落里。

太太来给丈夫端热麦酒乳饮，见那丫头在他身边风风火火地张罗，心里烦透了，便上楼进了客厅，气得嚎啕起来。

一清早，杂货商就在楼下高声喊着要他的长筒毛线袜。听得出他心里又烦躁又窝火，因为又得套上出海的上衣，蹬进挤脚的涉水靴，再出门到坏天气里去。

===Original===

He tore open the kitchen door, and asked them furiously how much longer they were going to keep him waiting.

But now his mouth grew as wide open as the doorway he stood in, and his face quite lit up with satisfaction.

Round about the walls, and in the warmth of the chimney corner, hung his sou’wester, and his oilskin jacket, and his trousers, and every blessed bit of clothes he was to put on, as dry as tinder. And in the middle of the kitchen bench he saw his large sea-boots standing there, so snug, and so nicely greased, that the grease ran right down the shafts and over the straps.

Such a servant for looking after him and taking care of him he had not believed it possible to get for love or money, cried the general dealer.

But now his wife could contain herself no longer. She showed him that the clothes were both scorched and burned, and that the whole of one side of the oilskin jacket was crumpled up with heat, and cracked if one pulled it never so lightly.

And in she dragged the big butter-keg, that he might see for himself how the wench had stuck both his boots in it and used it to grease them with.

===Chinese===

他猛地拽开厨房门，怒气冲冲地问他们还要让他等多久。

可这会儿他的嘴张得同他站着的门洞一样大，一张脸顿时喜得亮堂起来。

四面墙上，烟囱角落的暖处，挂着他的油布帽、油布外套、裤子，还有他身上要穿的每一件大大小小的衣物，干得像引火绒。厨房长凳正中，他看见他那双大号长筒水靴立在那儿，又服帖又滋润，抹的油多得上油顺着靴筒直往下淌，漫过了靴绊。

这样会照应人、体贴人的佣人，他从前不论求告还是使钱，都不信能雇得来，杂货商嚷道。

可太太这回再也忍不住了。她指给他看：那些衣裳不是烤焦就是烤煳了，油布外套整整一面皱缩成一团，轻轻一扯就裂开口子。

她又把那只大黄油桶拖进来，好让他亲眼瞧瞧，那丫头是怎么把他两只靴子都插进桶里、就着桶里的油给靴子上油的。

===Original===

But the general dealer stood there quite dumbfounded, and glanced now at the boots and now at the butter-tub.

He snapped his fingers, and his face twitched, and then he began to wipe away his tears.

He hastened to go in that they might not see that he was weeping.

“Mother does not know how kindly the wench has meant it all,” he sobbed. Good heavens! what if she *had* used butter for his boots, if she had only *meant* well. Never would he turn such a lass out of the house.

Then the wife gave it up altogether, and let the big kitchen wench rule as best she might. And it was not very long either before Toad let the key of the storeroom remain in the door from morn till eve. When anyone bawled out to her, “Who’s inside there?” she would simply answer, “It’s me!”

And she didn’t budge from the gingerbread-box, as she sat there and ate, even for Madame herself. But she always had an eye upon her master the general dealer.

===Chinese===

可杂货商呆呆地站在那里，眼睛一会儿瞟瞟靴子，一会儿又瞟瞟黄油桶。

他捻了个响指，脸抽搐了几下，接着便抹起眼泪来。

他赶紧走进里屋去，免得叫人看见他在哭。

“孩子他妈不晓得，这丫头可全是一片好心啊。”他抽抽搭搭地说。老天爷！就算她*当真*拿黄油抹了靴子，那又有什么要紧，只要她*是出于好意*。这样的丫头，他说什么也不会撵出家门。

太太索性彻底撒手，任凭这大个子厨房丫头当家做主。没多久，蟾蜍干脆让库房的钥匙从早到晚插在门上。谁朝里头喊一声：“里面是谁？”她照例只答一句：“是我！”

她坐在姜饼箱跟前吃着，一动不动，连太太本人来了也不挪窝。可她那双眼睛，始终盯着老爷杂货商。

===Original===

But he only jested with her, and asked her if she got food enough, and said that he was afraid he would, one day, find her starved to death.

Towards Christmas time, when folks were making ready to go a-fishing, Madame was busy betimes and bustled about as usual, and got the great cauldron taken down into the working-room for washing and wool-stamping.

The cooks hired for the occasion rolled out the *lefser*,45 and baked and frizzled on the flat oven-pans. And they brought in herring kegs from the shop, and meal and meat, both cured and fresh, and weighed and measured, and laid in stores of provisions.

But then it seemed to Toad as if she hadn’t a moment’s peace for prying into pots and pans. Her mistress was going backwards and forwards continually, between storeroom and pantry, after meal, or sugar, or butter, or syrup for the *lefser*. The storeroom door was ajar for her all day long.

So at last Toad grew downright wild. She was determined to put an end to all this racket. So she took it upon her to well smear the threshold of the storeroom with green soap.

Next morning her mistress came bustling along first thing with butter and a wooden ladle in a bowl, and she slipped and fell in the opening between the stairs and the storehouse door.

===Chinese===

他只跟她打趣，问她饭食够不够吃，还说只怕哪天要发现她活活饿死了。

临近圣诞节，众人张罗着出海打鱼，太太也照例一早忙活开了，把那口大锅搬进操作间，用来洗衣裳和踩洗羊毛。

临时雇来的厨娘们擀着雷夫瑟（lefser）45，在平底烤盘上又烤又煎。她们还从铺子里搬来一桶桶鲱鱼，还有面粉和肉——腌的鲜的都有——称的称，量的量，囤下各色口粮。

可蟾蜍只觉得这一来，她连窥探锅碗瓢盆的一点儿闲工夫都没有了。太太整日在库房和食品间之间来来回回，取面粉、取糖、取黄油、取抹雷夫瑟的糖浆，库房的门成天为她敞着一条缝。

蟾蜍终于发了野性。她打定主意要了结这番吵嚷，便自作主张，拿绿皂把库房门槛厚厚地抹了一层。

第二天一早，太太头一件事就是端着盛了黄油和木勺的碗匆匆走来，一脚踩滑，摔在楼梯口和库房门之间的过道里。

===Original===

There she lay till Toad dragged her up.

She carried her in to her husband with such a crying and yelling that it was heard all over the depot. Madame had been regularly worrying herself to death with all this bustle, said she, and now the poor soul had fallen and broken her leg.

But the one who cried the most, and didn’t know what to do with himself when he heard such weeping and wailing over his wife, was the general dealer.

None knew the real worth of that kitchen wench, said he.

And so it was Toad who now superintended everything, and both dispensed the stores and made provision for the household.

===Chinese===

她就那么躺着，直到蟾蜍把她拽起来。

蟾蜍把她抱到丈夫跟前，哭喊得惊天动地，整个货栈都听见了。太太天天为这摊子事操心，简直要把命操没了，她说，这会儿这可怜的人摔断了腿。

可哭得最凶的，听见有人这样为他的妻子哭天抹泪便六神无主的，还是杂货商自己。

谁也不识得这厨房丫头的真正价值，他说。

于是从这时候起，样样都归蟾蜍掌管：库房由她发放，全家的吃用由她筹办。

===Original===

She drove all the hired cooks and pancake rollers out of the house﻿—they were only eating her master out of house and home, she said.

The *lefser* were laid together without any syrup between them, and she gave out fat instead of butter. She distributed it herself, and packed it up in their *Nistebommers*.46

Never had the general dealer known the heavy household business disposed of so quickly as it was that year. He was quite astonished.

And he was really dumbfounded when Toad took him up into the storeroom, and showed him how little had been consumed, and how the cured shoulders of mutton and the hams hung down from the rafters in rows and rows.

“So long as things went on as they were going now,” said he, “she should have the control of the household like mother herself,” for his wife was now bedridden in her room upstairs.

===Chinese===

她把临时雇来的厨娘和摊煎饼的统统撵出了家门——她们只会把老爷的家底吃个精光，她说。

雷夫瑟一张张摞起来，中间半点糖浆也不夹；黄油也换成了油脂发放。她亲手一份份分下去，替她们装进各自的尼斯特博默（Nistebommers）46 饭包里。

这副沉重的家计，杂货商从没见哪一年操办得像那年一样快当。他着实吃了一惊。

待蟾蜍领他上库房，指给他看吃用何其节省，腌羊前腿和火腿一排排从椽子上垂挂下来，他简直惊得说不出话。

“只要日子照现在这样过下去，”他说，“家里就让她掌管，跟孩子他妈本人当家一样。”——因为他的妻子如今已卧病在楼上的房间里。

===Original===

And at Yuletide Toad baked and roasted, and cut things down so finely that her fellow-servants were almost driven to chew their wooden spoons and gnaw bones.

But such fat calves, and such ribs of pork, and such *lefser* filled with both syrup and butter, and such *mölje*47 and splendid fare for the guests that came to his house at Christmas-time the general dealer had never seen before.

Then the general dealer took her by the arm, and right down into the shop they both went together.

She might take what she would, said he, both of kirtles and neckerchiefs and other finery, so that she might dress and go in and out as if she were mother herself; and she might provide herself with beads and silk as much as she liked. There was nothing that she might not have.

But when the bailiff and the sexton sat at cards, and Toad came in to lay the tablecloth, they were like to have rolled off their chairs. Such a sight they had never seen before. Toad had rigged herself up with all manner of parti-coloured ’kerchiefs, and trimmed her hairy poll with blue and yellow and green ribbons till it looked like a carthorse’s tail. But they said nothing, for the sake of the general dealer, who thought she looked so smart, and was calling her in continually.

===Chinese===

到了尤尔节（Yuletide），蟾蜍又烤又烙，把吃食克扣得精细入微，同伴们险些被逼得去嚼木勺、啃骨头。

可那么肥的牛犊，那么好的猪肋，那么糖浆黄油夹足的雷夫瑟，那么香的摩尔耶（mölje）47，还有款待圣诞节来客的这许多盛馔，杂货商从前连见都没见过。

随后杂货商挽起她的胳膊，两人一道径直下了铺子。

裙衫也好，头巾也好，别的什么漂亮穿戴也好，她想要什么只管拿，他说，好让她打扮起来进进出出，俨然太太本人一般；珠子和绸子，她爱置办多少置办多少。没有什么是她拿不得的。

可治安官（bailiff）和教堂司事（sexton）坐着打牌，蟾蜍进来铺桌布时，两人差点从椅子上滚下来。这样的景象他们从未见过。蟾蜍把自己装扮得五花十色，满头杂色头巾，那颗毛茸茸的脑袋缠满了蓝的黄的绿的缎带，活像一匹拉车马的尾巴。可他们一声也没吭——看在杂货商的面上；老爷只觉得她打扮得真俊气，还一个劲儿把她往屋里叫。

===Original===

And they were forced to confess that the wench spared neither meat nor ale nor brandy. And on the third evening, when they got so drunk that they lay there like logs, she carried them off to bed as if they were sucking babes.

And so it went on, with feasting and entertaining, right up to the twentieth day after Christmas Day, and beyond it.

And that wench Toad used to smirk and stare about the room; and whenever they didn’t laugh or jest enough with her, she would plant herself right in the middle of the floor, and turn herself about in all her finery to attract notice, and say, “It’s me!”

And when the guests left the house they must needs admit that the general dealer was right when he said that such serving-maids were not to be picked up every day.

But those folks who went a-fishing for the general dealer, and had their provisions put up for them beforehand, were not slow to mark that Toad had the control of the shop and stores likewise.

===Chinese===

他们也不得不承认，这丫头在肉食、麦酒和白兰地上毫不小气。第三天晚上，两人喝得烂醉，直挺挺躺在那儿像两根木头，她把他们一个个抱上床去，像抱吃奶的娃娃。

就这样大宴小请，一直宴饮到圣诞节后的第二十天，还要更往后。

蟾蜍那丫头惯会嬉皮笑脸地朝满屋子人张望；每逢众人对她的笑闹打趣不够热络，她便径自往地板正中一站，浑身的盛装原地打转，非要惹人注目不可，嘴里说着：“是我！”

客人们告辞出门时，也只好承认杂货商说得在理：这样的使女，可不是天天都挑得着的。

可那些替杂货商出海打鱼、口粮预先由铺里打包好的人，也很快看出：铺子和库房，同样落在了蟾蜍手里。

===Original===

So it happened as might only have been expected. Their provisions ran short, and they had to return home just as the cod was biting best, while all the other fishermen sailed further out and made first-rate hauls.

The general dealer was like to have had apoplexy on the day that he saw his boats lying empty by the bridge in the height of the fishing season. His men came up in a body to the shop, headed by their eldest foreman, and laid a complaint before him.

The food that had been packed into their boxes and baskets, they said, couldn’t be called human food at all. The *lefser* were so hard, they said, that it was munch munch all day; there was only rancid fat on them, with scarcely a glimpse of bacon; and as for the cured shoulders of mutton, one had scarcely shaved off a thin slice when one scraped against the bare bone.

Up into the storeroom went the general dealer like a shot.

But as for Toad, she smote her hands above her head, and said that it was as much as he, the general dealer, could manage, to meet the heavy expenses for fishhooks and fish-baskets, and nets and lines, without having to provide his fishermen with salt herring and bacon, and fresh butter and *lefser* and ground coffee into the bargain. They had no need to starve when they had all the fish of the sea right under their noses, said she.

===Chinese===

于是事情只落了个大家意料中的下场。他们的口粮见了底，只好在鳕鱼正咬钩咬得最欢的当口回家；而别的渔船都驶向更远的海面，捕得了头等的好收成。

渔汛正旺的那几天，杂货商眼看自己的船空空地泊在桥边，差点中了风。他的伙计们成群结队上到铺子来，为首的是他们年事最高的领班，当着他的面诉起苦来。

装进他们箱里筐里的那些吃食，他们说，根本算不上人吃的东西。雷夫瑟硬得很，他们说，成天嚼啊嚼的，嘴里只是咔嚓咔嚓；上头只抹了点哈喇了的油脂，腌肉星子几乎见不着；至于腌羊前腿呢，薄薄一片还没削下来，刀就蹭着光骨头了。

杂货商一个箭步奔上了库房。

可蟾蜍呢，双手往头顶上一拍，说道：鱼钩鱼篓、渔网钓线这些大宗开销，他杂货商东家撑着就已经够受了，哪里还供得起渔工们的咸鲱鱼和腌肉，外带鲜黄油、雷夫瑟和磨好的咖啡。鼻子底下就是满海的鱼，他们犯不着挨饿，她说。

===Original===

And then she handed him, as a specimen, one of his own *lefser*, which she had filled with butter and syrup herself, and let him taste it. And he tasted it, and ate and ate till the syrup ran down both corners of his mouth. Such good greasy *lefser* he had never tasted before.

Then the general dealer gave them a bit of his mind.

He was as red as a turkey-cock; and out of the shop-door they went head first﻿—some three yards and some four, according as he got a good grip of them; and old Thore, who had steered the big fembøring, both for him and his father, was discharged.

But Kjel, the herdsman, had hid himself out of the way up on the threshing-floor whilst the row was going on, and the general dealer was shrieking and bellowing his worst in the yard below. And he stood there and peeped through the little window. Then he saw his mistress, who hadn’t been out of bed for nine weeks, hobble forward and stare out of her bedroom window.

===Chinese===

接着她递给他一张样品——他自家的雷夫瑟，她亲手抹上了黄油和糖浆——请他尝尝。他便尝了，一吃就吃个不住，吃得糖浆顺着两边嘴角往下淌。这样油润喷香的雷夫瑟，他从来没尝过。

于是杂货商把他们狠狠数落了一顿。

他脸红得像只公火鸡；伙计们一个个头朝前飞出铺子门——他抓得顺手的飞出三码，抓得更顺的飞出四码；老托雷（Thore）——给他父子两代掌过大芬伯艇舵的——也给辞退了。

可牧人凯尔（Kjel）趁乱早躲到了上头的脱谷场上——那时底下正闹作一团，杂货商在院子里骂得声嘶力竭。他站在那儿，从小窗里往外偷看，只见太太——她九个星期没下过床了——一瘸一拐地挪上前来，朝卧室窗外呆望。

===Original===

She took on terribly, and cried and wrung her thin hands when she saw their old foreman told to go to the devil, and shamble off with his cap in his hand as if he were deranged.

But she dared not so much as shout a word of comfort after him, for there stood Toad, big and broad, in the storehouse door, with a platter of *mölje* in her hand, and shook her fist after him.

Then Kjel was like to have wept too.﻿ ﻿… That stout Toad should not grease herself shiny with *mölje* fat much longer in *their* house, or he’d know the reason why, thought Kjel.

And from thenceforth Kjel kept a strict watch upon her. There were lots of things going on that he couldn’t make out at all.

===Chinese===

她见他们年迈的领班被人骂去见鬼，手里捏着帽子、失魂落魄地拖着步子走远，登时伤透了心，哭起来，绞着自己那双瘦手。

可她连一句宽慰的话都不敢朝他喊——库房门口正立着高大魁梧的蟾蜍，手里端着一盘摩尔耶，朝他的背影挥拳头。

凯尔也险些哭出来。……那肥壮的蟾蜍休想再在*他们*家里拿摩尔耶的油把自己抹得油光水滑，不然他定要叫她知道厉害，凯尔心想。

打那时起，凯尔便对她严加盯视。家里有许多蹊跷事，他怎么也参不透。

===Original===

Towards springtime, when they put the mast into the large new yacht which was to take the first trading voyage to Bergen, the general dealer was so glad that he was running up and down from the bridge to the house the whole day. He had never imagined that the yacht would have turned out so fine and stately.

And when they had the tackle and the shrouds all ready, and were hoisting away at the yards, he spun round on his heel and snapped his fingers﻿—“That lass Toad should go with him to Bergen,” said he.﻿ ﻿… “She had never seen the town, poor thing! while as for mother, she had been there three times already.”

But it seemed to Kjel that he saw more in this than other people saw.

As for Toad, when she heard she was to go to Bergen, she regularly turned the house upside down. There was nothing good enough for her in the whole shop; there was not a shelf that she didn’t ransack to find the finery and frippery that glittered most.

===Chinese===

开春时节，众人给那艘新造的大帆船立桅——它要跑头一趟去卑尔根的买卖——杂货商高兴得从桥边到屋子来回奔走了一整天。他从来没有想到，这帆船会造得这样漂亮、这样气派。

索具和支索都备妥、横桁往上吊的时候，他脚跟一旋转，手指打了个响——“蟾蜍那丫头该跟我一道去卑尔根，”他说。……“可怜的丫头，她还没见过城呢！至于孩子他妈，她都去过三回了。”

可凯尔总觉得，这里头的名堂比旁人瞧见的要多。

至于蟾蜍，一听说要去卑尔根，登时把整座宅子翻了个底朝天。满铺子里没有一样东西配得上她；哪一层货架她没翻过，专拣那些最闪闪发亮的衣裳首饰和零碎儿。

===Original===

And in the evening, when the others had lain them down to rest, she strolled over to the storehouse with a light.

But Kjel, who was a very light sleeper, was up and after her in an instant, and peeped at her through the crack in the door.

There he saw her cutting up the victuals and putting one titbit aside after the other, *lefser* and sweet-cakes and bacon and collared-beef, into the large chest which she had hidden behind the herring barrels. And on this, the last evening before their departure for Bergen, she had filled her provision-chest so full that she had to sit upon it, with all her huge heavy weight, to press it down.

But the lock wouldn’t catch; she had filled the chest too full, so she had to get up and stamp backwards on the lid till it regularly thundered; and sure enough she forced it down at last.

===Chinese===

到了晚上，众人都躺下歇息了，她却点着一盏灯，慢悠悠踱到库房去。

凯尔觉轻，一骨碌爬起来跟了上去，贴着门缝往里偷看。

他看见她正在切吃食，一样样美味往旁边搁——雷夫瑟、甜点心、腌肉、卷制牛肉——都收进那只藏在鲱鱼桶后头的大箱子里。这天正是动身去卑尔根前的最后一晚，她把干粮箱塞得这样满，不得不坐上去，用她那庞大沉重的身子把箱盖压下去。

可锁扣不上；箱子塞得太满了，她只好站起来，倒退着往箱盖上跺，跺得咚咚如雷；到末了，她终于硬是把盖子压了下去。

===Original===

But the heel she stamped down upon it with was much more like the hoof of a horse than the foot of a human being, thought Kjel.

Then she carried the chest to the wagon that it might be smuggled on board without anyone seeing it. After that she went into the stable and unloosed the horse. But then there was a pretty to do in the stable!

The horse knew that there was witchcraft afoot, and would not allow itself to be inspanned. Toad dragged and dragged, and the horse shied and kicked. At last the wench used her back-legs, just as a mare does.

Such sport as that no human eye should have ever seen.

===Chinese===

只是，她跺箱盖用的那只后脚跟，与其说像人的脚，还不如说像马的蹄子，凯尔心想。

随后她把箱子搬上板车，好叫人不知鬼不觉地弄上船。完事她走进马厩，去解那匹马。可马厩里登时闹翻了天！

那马知道有妖法作祟，死活不肯上套。蟾蜍拽了又拽，马又躲又踢。到末了，这丫头使起后腿来，活脱脱是匹母马的架势。

这样的把戏，本不该叫任何活人的眼睛瞧见。

===Original===

And straight off to the general dealer rushed Kjel, and got him to come out with him.

There in the moonshine that wench, Toad, and the dun horse were flinging out at each other as if for a wager, so that their hoofs dashed against the framework of the stable-door. Their long legs flew in turn over the stable walls, and the sparks scattered about in showers.

Then the general dealer grew all of a shiver and staggered about. Blood flew from his nose, and Kjel had to help him into the kitchen and duck his head in the sink. That night the general dealer didn’t go to bed at all; but he walked up and down and stamped till the floor regularly thundered. And it was scarcely light next morning when he sent off Kjel with a dollar in his fist to old Thore the foreman. And he sent in the same way to all the boat people down by the shore.

Thore was told to put on his holiday clothes and get out the fembøring, and row Madame herself to the yacht with the last lading. She should go with him to Bergen. There she should get both a silk dress and a shawl, and a gold watch and chain into the bargain, and engage a Bergen serving-wench.

It was still early in the day when the yacht lay in the bay with her flag flying, all ready to start.

===Chinese===

凯尔一溜烟奔去找杂货商，把他拽了出来。

月光底下，蟾蜍那丫头和那匹灰褐马正你来我往地对踢，像赌了东道似的，蹄子撞得厩门框砰砰直响。两条长腿轮番飞过马厩的墙头，火星四溅，泼洒如雨。

杂货商顿时浑身一个寒噤，站立不稳，鼻血都溅了出来。凯尔只得搀他进厨房，把他的头按到水槽里浸了浸。那天夜里，杂货商压根儿没上床，只是走来走去地跺脚，跺得地板咚咚如雷。第二天早晨天光还没大亮，他就打发凯尔攥着一块银元，给领班老托雷送去；岸边船上的人等，他也一一照样打发人送了。

他托老托雷穿上节日的衣裳，把芬伯艇划出来，亲自送太太上帆船，载着最后一批货。太太要随他同去卑尔根，在那儿添一条绸裙、一方披巾，外加一块带链的金怀表，再雇一个卑尔根使女。

那天时辰还早，帆船已经泊在湾里，旗子飘扬，一切齐备，只待启程。

===Original===

When they had hoisted the sail, that wench Toad, heavy and stout, came, puffing and blowing, across the bridge, in full parade, with rings on all her sprawling fingers, and her body covered with all the yellow and green and red ribbons she could possibly find room for on her ample person.

There she stood waiting for them to come back in the stately fembøring and take her on board.

And when they began to raise the anchor, and the general dealer appeared on deck with his large meerschaum pipe and his telescope, she smirked and minced and wriggled and twisted, and cried aloud, “It’s me!”

She thought he wanted to peep at her splendour through his spyglass.

===Chinese===

帆升起来之后，蟾蜍那丫头拖着肥壮的身子，气喘吁吁地过了桥，一身盛装游行的架势：叉开的手指上戴满了戒指，宽阔的身躯上，黄的绿的红的缎带，能挂的地方都挂满了。

她站在那儿，只等他们乘着那条气派的芬伯艇回来，接她上船。

待他们开始起锚，杂货商叼着大海泡石烟斗、拿着望远镜在甲板上露面时，她又是媚笑又是扭捏，转过来拧过去，扯开嗓子高喊：“是我！”

她只当他是要拿望远镜窥看她的荣光。

===Original===

All at once she saw Madame standing by his side in full travelling costume, and understood that they were going away without her.

Then she kicked out so that the planks of the bridge groaned and creaked beneath her. Eight into the sea she plunged, and caught hold of the anchor, and tugged and held the ship back till the cable broke.

Then head over heels she went with both her hoofs in the air.

But the yacht glided away under full sail, and the general dealer stood there and laughed till he nearly fell overboard.

===Chinese===

蓦地，她看见太太一身远行的装束立在他身边，顿时明白：他们要丢下她走了。

她蹬起脚来，桥板在下面呻吟作响。她纵身跳进海里，一把抓住铁锚，又拖又拽，死死拖住船不放，直到锚缆绷断。

接着她一个倒栽葱翻了下去，两只马蹄冲天。

帆船却张满帆滑走了，杂货商站在那儿，直笑得险些跌进海里。

## The Cormorants of Andvaer / 安德沃尔的鸬鹚

===Original===

Outside Andvaer lies an island, the haunt of wild birds, which no man can land upon, be the sea never so quiet; the sea-swell girds it round about with sucking whirlpools and dashing breakers.

On fine summer days something sparkles there through the sea-foam like a large gold ring; and, time out of mind, folks have fancied there was a treasure there left by some pirates of old.

At sunset, sometimes, there looms forth from thence a vessel with a castle astern, and a glimpse is caught now and then of an old-fashioned galley. There it lies as if in a tempest, and carves its way along through heavy white rollers.

Along the rocks sit the cormorants in a long black row, lying in wait for dogfish.

But there was a time when one knew the exact number of these birds. There was never more nor less of them than twelve, while upon a stone, out in the sea-mist, sat the thirteenth, but it was only visible when it rose and flew right over the island.

===Chinese===

安德沃尔（Andvaer）外海横着一座小岛，是野鸟出没之地，任凭海面怎样平静，也没有人能登上它；海涌四下环着它，尽是吸人的漩涡和扑溅的碎浪。

晴好的夏日里，有什么东西在泡沫底下闪闪发亮，像一枚硕大的金戒指；打远古以来，人们便猜想那里藏着旧时海盗留下的财宝。

有时日落时分，那边会隐隐现出一艘船尾带着楼堡的大船，间或还瞥得见一条古旧的大桨帆船。它仿佛正处在风暴之中，在沉重的白色巨浪间劈出一条航路。

礁石上蹲着鸬鹚，排成长长的一列黑影，潜伏着守候角鲨。

可是曾有一个时期，这些鸟的确切数目人人皆知。它们从来不多不少，恰好十二只；而在海雾深处的一块石头上，坐着第十三只，只是要等它飞起来、径直掠过岛顶的时候，才看得见它。

===Original===

The only persons who lived near the Vær32 at winter time, long after the fishing season was over, was a woman and a slip of a girl. Their business was to guard the scaffolding poles for drying fish against the birds of prey, who had such a villainous trick of hacking at the drying-ropes.

The young girl had thick coal-black hair, and a pair of eyes that peeped at folk so oddly. One might almost have said that she was like the cormorants outside there, and she had never seen much else all her life. Nobody knew who her father was.

Thus they lived till the girl had grown up.

It was found that, in the summer time, when the fishermen went out to the Vær to fetch away the dried fish, that the young fellows began underbidding each other, so as to be selected for that special errand.

===Chinese===

渔汛早已收场许久，冬天还住在渔村（Vær）32近旁的，只有一个女人和一个瘦小的姑娘。她们的营生是看守晾鱼架的木杆，防备猛禽——那些家伙有个恶劣透顶的习惯，专爱啄断晾鱼的绳索。

那姑娘生着一头浓密的煤黑头发，一双眼睛看人的神气古怪得很。几乎可以说，她活像外面那些鸬鹚——她这一辈子见过的，此外也没有多少别的东西。谁也不知道她的父亲是谁。

她们就这样过活，直到姑娘长大成人。

后来人们发觉，每到夏天，渔民们出海到渔村来运干鱼的时候，年轻汉子们便开始互相压价，好争得这趟特别的差遣。

===Original===

Some gave up their share of profits, and others their wages; and there was a general complaint in all the villages round about that on such occasions no end of betrothals were broken off.

But the cause of it all was the girl out yonder with the odd eyes.

For all her rough and ready ways, she had something about her, said those she chatted with, that there was no resisting. She turned the heads of all the young fellows; it seemed as if they couldn’t live without her.

The first winter a lad wooed her who had both house and warehouse of his own.

“If you come again in the summer time, and give me the right gold ring I will be wedded by, something may come of it,” said she.

===Chinese===

有的让出自己的一份分红，有的连工钱也不要；四近各村一片抱怨，说每到这种时节，不知有多少婚约就此告吹。

而这一切的根由，就是远处那个长着古怪眼睛的姑娘。

同她闲聊过的人都说，别看她粗手粗脚、随随便便，她身上却有一样东西，叫人无法抗拒。她把年轻汉子的头脑统统搅昏了；他们仿佛离了她就活不成。

头一个冬天来向她求婚的，是个自己有房子、有货栈的小伙子。

「要是你夏天再来，把我成婚该戴的那枚金戒指给我带来，兴许能成。」她说。

===Original===

And, sure enough, in the summer time the lad was there again.

He had a lot of fish to fetch away, and she might have had a gold ring as heavy and as bonnie as heart could wish for.

“The ring I must have lies beneath the wreckage, in the iron chest, over at the island yonder,” said she; “that is, if you love me enough to dare fetch it.”

But then the lad grew pale.

He saw the sea-bore rise and fall out there like a white wall of foam on the bright warm summer day, and on the island sat the cormorants sleeping in the sunshine.

===Chinese===

果不其然，到了夏天，那小伙子又来了。

他有一大批鱼要运走；她满可以得着一枚金戒指，要多沉有多沉，要多漂亮有多漂亮，随心如意。

「我一定要的那枚戒指，就躺在那边岛上沉船残骸底下的一只铁箱子里，」她说；「要是你爱我爱得够深，敢去把它取来的话。」

可这下小伙子脸色发白了。

他望见外面的海涌一起一伏，像一堵白沫筑成的墙，立在这明亮和暖的夏日里；岛上，鸬鹚正蹲在阳光下沉睡。

===Original===

“Dearly do I love thee,” said he, “but such a quest as that would mean my burial, not my bridal.”

The same instant the thirteenth cormorant rose from his stone in the misty foam, and flew right over the island.

Next winter the steersman of a yacht came a wooing. For two years he had gone about and hugged his misery for her sake, and he got the same answer.

“If you come again in the summer time, and give me the right gold ring I will be wedded with, something may come of it.”

===Chinese===

「我深深地爱你，」他说，「可这样一趟寻访，等来的将是我的葬礼，而不是我的婚礼。」

就在这同一瞬，第十三只鸬鹚在迷蒙的雾沫中从它的石头上飞起，径直掠过岛顶。

第二年冬天来求婚的，是一艘游艇上的舵手。为了她，他两年来失魂落魄地四处游荡，紧抱着那份苦楚，得到的回答却一模一样。

「要是你夏天再来，把我成婚该戴的那枚金戒指给我带来，兴许能成。」

===Original===

Out to the Vær he came again on Midsummer Day.

But when he heard where the gold ring lay, he sat and wept the whole day till evening, when the sun began to dance northwestward into the sea.

Then the thirteenth cormorant arose, and flew right over the island.

There was nasty weather during the third winter.

===Chinese===

仲夏日那天，他又出海来到渔村。

可是一听那金戒指躺在什么地方，他便坐在那里哭了一整天，直到傍晚太阳开始朝着西北方舞进海里。

于是第十三只鸬鹚飞了起来，径直掠过岛顶。

第三个冬天，天气险恶。

===Original===

There were manifold wrecks, and on the keel of a boat, which came driving ashore, hung an exhausted young lad by his knife-belt.

But they couldn’t get the life back into him, roll and rub him about in the boathouse as they might.

Then the girl came in.

“ ’Tis my bridegroom!” said she.

And she laid him in her bosom, and sat with him the whole night through, and put warmth into his heart.

===Chinese===

那年沉船无数；一条被冲上岸来的小船，龙骨上挂着个精疲力竭的年轻人，全凭佩刀的皮带吊在那里。

可任凭人们在船屋里怎样翻转他、揉搓他，也没法把生命弄回他身上。

这时姑娘走了进来。

「这是我的新郎！」她说。

她把他搂进怀里，陪他坐了整整一夜，把暖意渡进他的心口。

===Original===

And when the morning came, his heart beat.

“Methought I lay betwixt the wings of a cormorant, and leaned my head against its downy breast,” said he.

The lad was ruddy and handsome, with curly hair, and he couldn’t take his eyes away from the girl.

He took work upon the Vær.

But off he must needs be gadding and chatting with her, be it never so early and never so late.

===Chinese===

清晨来临，他的心脏跳动了。

「我恍恍惚惚觉得自己躺在一只鸬鹚的双翅之间，头靠着它绒茸茸的胸脯。」他说。

这小伙子面色红润，相貌英俊，一头鬈发，眼睛怎么也离不开那姑娘。

他便在渔村里揽下了活计。

可他总免不了要凑到她身边游荡闲谈，任凭天多早、多晚。

===Original===

So it fared with him as it had fared with the others.

It seemed to him that he could not live without her, and on the day when he was bound to depart, he wooed her.

“*Thee* I will not fool,” said she. “Thou hast lain on my breast, and I would give my life to save thee from sorrow. Thou shalt have me if thou wilt place the betrothal ring upon my finger; but longer than the day lasts thou canst not keep me. And now I will wait, and long after thee with a horrible longing, till the summer comes.”

On Midsummer Day the youth came thither in his boat all alone.

===Chinese===

他的遭际，和先前那些人一模一样。

他觉得自己离了她活不成；到了必须启程的那一天，他向她求了婚。

「*你*，我是不愿诓骗的，」她说。「你在我怀里躺过，为了不叫你悲伤，我连性命都肯舍。你若肯把订婚戒指戴到我手指上，我就归你；只是白昼有多长，你便只能留我多久。如今我要等待，怀着可怕的渴望苦苦想你，直到夏天来临。」

仲夏日那天，年轻人独自一人乘着船来到这里。

===Original===

Then she told him of the ring that he must fetch for her from among the skerries.

“If thou hast taken me off the keel of a boat, thou mayest cast me forth yonder again,” said the lad. “Live without thee I cannot.”

But as he laid hold of the oars in order to row out, she stepped into the boat with him and sat in the stern. Wondrous fair was she!

It was beautiful summer weather, and there was a swell upon the sea: wave followed upon wave in long bright rollers.

===Chinese===

于是她把那枚戒指的事告诉他：他必须到礁岛群中为她取来。

「你既从船的龙骨上把我救了下来，也尽可以把我再抛回那边海里去，」小伙子说。「离了你，我活不成。」

可当他握住双桨、正要划出海去时，她也跨进船来，坐在船尾。她美丽得出奇！

正是明媚的夏日天气，海上起着涌浪：波涛一浪接着一浪，铺成长长一道道明亮的浪脊。

===Original===

The lad sat there, lost in the sight of her, and he rowed and rowed till the insucking breakers roared and thundered among the skerries; the ground-swell was strong, and the frothing foam spurted up as high as towers.

“If thy life is dear to thee, turn back now,” said she.

“Thou art dearer to me than life itself,” he made answer.

But just as it seemed to the lad as if the prow were going under, and the jaws of death were gaping wide before him, it grew all at once as still as a calm, and the boat could run ashore as if there was never a billow there.

On the island lay a rusty old ship’s anchor half out of the sea.

===Chinese===

小伙子坐在那里，望着她出了神；他划呀划，一直划到吸人的碎浪在礁岛间轰吼如雷的地方；海底的涌浪强劲，翻腾的泡沫高高喷起，如同塔楼。

「你若惜命，现在就回头。」她说。

「你比我的性命本身还贵重。」他答道。

可就在小伙子觉得船头仿佛就要没顶、死亡的巨口正朝他大张的时候，四下忽然静得像全然无风的海面，船顺顺当当地靠上了岸，仿佛这里从来不曾有过半个浪头。

岛上横着一只锈迹斑斑的旧船锚，半个浸在海里。

===Original===

“In the iron chest which lies beneath the anchor is my dowry,” said she; “carry it up into thy boat, and put the ring that thou seest on my finger. With this thou dost make me thy bride. So now I am thine till the sun dances northwestwards into the sea.”

It was a gold ring with a red stone in it, and he put it on her finger and kissed her.

In a cleft on the skerry was a patch of green grass. There they sat them down, and they were ministered to in wondrous wise, how he knew not nor cared to know, so great was his joy.

“Midsummer Day is beauteous,” said she, “and I am young and thou art my bridegroom. And now we’ll to our bridal bed.”

===Chinese===

「锚底下那只铁箱子里装的，就是我的嫁妆，」她说；「把它搬上你的船，再把你见到的那枚戒指戴到我手指上。凭着它，你便立我为你新娘。所以现在，直到太阳朝西北舞进海里，我都是你的。」

那是一枚镶着红宝石的金戒指，他把它戴上她的手指，吻了她。

礁岛的一道石缝里有一小片青草地。他们在那里坐了下来；有种种奇妙的方式伺候着他们——他是怎样受款待的，他不知道，也不想知道，他的欢喜实在太大。

「仲夏日真美，」她说，「我又年轻，你又是我的新郎。现在，我们到我们的婚床上去吧。」

===Original===

So bonnie was she that he could not contain himself for love.

But when night drew nigh, and the sun began to dance out into the sea, she kissed him and shed tears.

“Beauteous is the summer day,” said she, “and still more beauteous is the summer evening; but now the dusk cometh.”

===Chinese===

她美成那样，他爱得情难自持。

可是夜色临近，太阳开始朝着海里舞落下去时，她吻了他，落下泪来。

「夏日白昼真美，」她说，「夏日傍晚更美；可现在，暮色降临了。」

===Original===

And all at once it seemed to him as if she were becoming older and older and fading right away.

When the sun went below the sea-margin there lay before him on the skerry some mouldering linen rags and nought else.

Calm was the sea, and in the clear Midsummer night there flew *twelve* cormorants out over the sea.

===Chinese===

猛然间，他觉得她仿佛越来越老、越来越老，径直消散得无影无踪。

太阳沉到海际之下时，礁岛上躺在他面前的，只有几片朽烂的亚麻布碎片，别无他物。

海面平静，澄澈的仲夏夜里，有*十二*只鸬鹚飞出海去。

## “The Earth Draws” / “大地在拉扯”

===Original===
There was once a young salesman at the storekeeper’s at Sörvaag.
He was fair, with curly hair, shrewd blue eyes, and so smart, and obliging, and handsome, that all the girls in the town got themselves sent on errands, and made pilgrimages to the shop on purpose to see him. Moreover, he was so smart and skilful in everything he put his hand to, that the storekeeper never would part with him.
Now it happened one day that he went out to a fishing station for his principal.
The current was dead against him, so he rode close in shore.

===Chinese===
从前，瑟尔沃格（Sörvaag）的店主铺子里有一个年轻的伙计。
他生得白净，一头鬈发，一双精明的蓝眼睛，又伶俐、又殷勤、又俊俏，镇上的姑娘们个个设法寻些差事出门，专程跑到铺子里来看他。况且他不论上手什么活计都那样麻利精熟，店主怎么也不肯放他走。
一天，他替东家出门，到一个渔站去办事。
潮流死死地顶着他的船，他便紧贴着岸边行船。

===Original===
All at once he saw a little ring in the rocky wall a little above high-water mark. He thought it was the sort of ring which is used for fastening boats to, so he fancied it wouldn’t do any harm to rest a bit and lay to ashore, and have a snack of something, for he had been pulling at the oars from early morn.
But when he took hold of the ring to run his boatline through it, it fitted round his finger so tightly that he had to tug at it. He tugged, and out of the mountain side with a rush came a large drawer. It was brimful of silk neckerchiefs and women’s frippery.
He was amazed, and began pondering the matter over.
Then he saw what looked like rusty flakes of iron in rows right over the whole mountain side, exactly resembling the slit of his own drawer.

===Chinese===
蓦地，他看见岩壁上、略高出高潮线的地方有个小小的环。他以为那是拴船用的那号环，心想靠岸歇上一会儿、垫点吃食想必不碍事——他可是从大清早起就一直摇着桨。
可当他攥住那环、想把船缆穿过去时，那环却紧紧箍上了他的手指，他只得使劲去拽。这一拽，山腰里哗地冲出一只大抽屉，满满当当塞着丝绸围巾和女人的花俏物件。
他又惊又疑，琢磨起这件事来。
接着他看见整面山坡上排着一道道铁锈色的薄片般的裂缝，同他那只抽屉的开口一模一样。

===Original===
He had now got the ring on his finger, and must needs try if it would open the other slits also. And out he drew drawer after drawer full of gold bracelets and silver bracelets, glass pearls, brooches and rings, bracelets and laced caps, yarn, nightcaps and woollen drawers, coffee, sugar, groats, tobacco pipes, buttons, hooks and eyes, knives, axes, and scythes.
He drew out drawer after drawer; there was no end to the display they made.
But all round about him he heard, as it were, the humming of a crowd and the tramp of sea-boots. There was a hubbub, as if they were rolling hogsheads over a bridge and hoisting sails against the wind, and out from the sea sounded the stroke of oars and the bumping of boats putting ashore.
Then he began to have an inkling that he had laid to his boat at a mooring-ring belonging to the underground folk, and had lit right upon their landing-place where they deposited their wares.

===Chinese===
如今那环已套上了手指，他不由得想试试它能否也拉开其余的裂缝。于是一只又一只抽屉被他拉了出来，满装着金镯银镯、玻璃珠子、胸针戒指、手镯和花边帽、纱线、睡帽和绒线衬裤，还有咖啡、糖、麦米、烟斗、纽扣、衣钩搭扣、刀子、斧头和长柄镰。
他一只接一只地往外拉抽屉，那琳琅满目的架势没有个尽头。
可在他四周，他仿佛听见人群的嗡嗡语声和长统水手靴的踏步声。闹哄哄一片，像是有人在桥上滚着大酒桶，逆着风扯帆，海面上又传来桨声阵阵、小船靠岸的磕碰声。
他这才隐隐明白过来：自己把船系在了地下人（the underground folk）的一个系船环上，恰好一头撞在他们存放货物的埠头。

===Original===
He stood there looking into a drawer of meerschaum pipes. They were finer than any he had thought it possible to find in the whole world.
Then he felt, as it were, the blow of a heavy hand which tried to thrust him aside; but, at the same time, someone laughed so merrily close by. The same instant he saw a young woman in the forepart of his boat. She was leaning, with broad shoulders and hairy arms, over a meal-sack. Her eyes laughed and shot forth sparks as from a smithy in the dark, but her face was oddly pale.
Then she vanished altogether like a vision.
He was glad when he got down into his boat again, and pushed off and rode away.

===Chinese===
他站在那里，望着一只盛满海泡石烟斗的抽屉。那些烟斗之精美，是他原以为走遍全天下也寻不着的。
这时他仿佛挨了一只沉甸甸的大手的一击，要把他推搡到一旁；可与此同时，近旁又有人笑得那样欢。同一瞬间，他看见自己船头站着个年轻女人。她肩膀宽阔，手臂毛茸茸的，正俯身对着一条面粉袋。她一双眼睛笑着，迸出火星，恰似黑暗中的锻炉，脸色却白得出奇。
随后她像幻影一般，整个人没了踪影。
等重新下到船里、撑船离岸划走，他才算高兴起来。

===Original===
But when he got out into the sound, and slackened speed a bit, he perceived that the ring still sat upon his finger.
His first thought was to tear it off and fling it into the sea; but then it sat tighter than ever.
It was so curiously wrought and fretted and engraved that he must needs examine it more curiously; and the longer he looked at it the stranger the gold whereof it was wrought gleamed and glistened. Turn it as he would to examine its spirals, he could never make out where they began and where they ended.
But as he sat there and looked and looked at it, the black crackling and sparkling eyes of that pale face stood out more and more plainly before his eyes. He didn’t exactly know whether he thought her ugly or handsome﻿—the uncanny creature!

===Chinese===
可等他划出海峡，稍稍放缓速度，才发觉那环仍旧套在手指上。
他头一个念头是把它撸下来扔进海里；可这一来它箍得反而更紧了。
这环的做工、镂花和錾纹如此奇巧，他不由得越发好奇地端详起来；看得越久，铸环的黄金便闪发出越发古怪的光泽。他把环转来转去细察那些螺旋纹路，却怎么也辨不清它们从何处起、到何处止。
可他坐在那里看了又看，那张苍白脸上的那双乌黑、噼啪迸着火花的眼睛，在他眼前越来越分明。他说不清自己究竟觉得她丑还是俊——那个邪气的生灵！

===Original===
The ring he now meant to keep, come what might.
And home he rowed, and said not a word to anybody of what had happened to him.
But from that day forth a strange restlessness came over him.
When he was sweeping out the shop or measuring goods, he would suddenly stand there in a brown study, and fancy he was right away at the landing-stage in the mountainside, and the black woman was laughing at him over the meal-sack.

===Chinese===
这环如今他是打定主意要留下了，无论出什么事都照留不误。
他划船回了家，对自己的遭遇一个字也没向人提起。
可从那一天起，一种古怪的不安缠上了他。
每逢他在铺子里扫地或是量货，会突然站住出了神，恍惚觉得自己又回到了山腰里那处埠头，那个黑眼睛的女人正伏在面粉袋上冲他笑。

===Original===
Out yonder he must needs venture once more, and put his ring to the test, though it cost him his life.
And in the course of the summer his boat lay over at the mountainside in the selfsame place as before.
When he had opened the drawer with his gold ring, he caught sight of the broad-shouldered woman. Her eyes sparkled, and had a wild look about them, and she peered curiously at him.
And, every time he came, he seemed to be more expected, and she was more and more gladsome. They became quite old acquaintances, and she was always waiting for him there.

===Chinese===
他必须再到那边去闯一回，试试他的环，哪怕搭上性命也在所不惜。
于是那年夏天，他的船又靠到了山腰下，还是原先那个地方。
他用金环拉开抽屉后，一眼便望见那个宽肩膀的女人。她双眼灼灼放光，透着一股野气，正好奇地打量他。
而且他每来一回，都显得更被人盼着，她也一次比一次欢喜。两人渐渐成了老相识，她总在那里等他。

===Original===
But at home he grew gloomy and silent. Yet, although he bethought him that it was all sorcery, and her arms were hairy almost like a beast’s, and although he determined and really tried to keep away, nevertheless he could not help going thither, and whenever he had been away from her a whole week, she grew quite unmanageable, and laughed and shrieked when she saw him coming again.
And he always heard the noise and the bustle of many people all about him, but never could he see anything. It seemed to him, however, as if they all lay a little way off and pulled their boat aside for him to pass. His boat, too, was always nicely baled out, and the oars and sails righted and trimmed. The cable, too, was fastened for him whenever he came, and thrown to him whenever he went away.
Now and then she so managed it that he caught a glimpse into their warehouses and their bright halls in the mountain side, and at such times she seemed to be enticing him after her.
